\displaystyle x_{1}\bar{x}_{1}+I=(x_{2}+\bar{x}_{2})+I

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle x_{1}\bar{x}_{1}-(x_{2}+\bar{x}_{2})\in I.

Set
y_{1}=x_{1}
and
y_{2}=x_{2}+(x_{1}\bar{x}_{1})/2-(x_{2}+\bar{x}_{2})/2
. Then
(y_{1},y_{2})\in\mathcal{A}(R)
, and clearly
(y_{1}+I,y_{2}+I)=(x_{1}+I,x_{2}+I)
, showing that
(y_{1},y_{2})\in\mathcal{A}(R)
serves as a pre-image of
(x_{1}+I,x_{2}+I)
. Hence, the natural map
\mathcal{A}(R)\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}(R/I)
is a surjective.

We now return to the assumption that
2
(resp.,
3
) is invertible in
R
, and verify condition
(A2)
. It is clear that
\mathfrak{m}_{\theta}\subset R_{\theta}\cap\mathfrak{m}_{\theta}R
. Let
x\in R_{\theta}\cap\mathfrak{m}_{\theta}R.
Then
x=\bar{x}
and
x=\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}x_{i}
where
m_{i}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\theta}
and
x_{i}\in R
. Define
y_{i}=(x_{i}+\bar{x}_{i})/2
(resp.,
y_{i}=(x_{i}+\bar{x}_{i}+\bar{\bar{x}}_{i})/3)
and set
y=\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}y_{i}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\theta}
. Then
x-y=\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}(x_{i}-y_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}(x_{i}-\bar{x_{i}})/2=(x-\bar{x})/2=0
(resp.,
x-y=\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}((x_{i}-\bar{x_{i}})+(x_{i}-\bar{\bar{x_{i}}}))/3=((x-\bar{x})+(x-\bar{\bar{x}}))/3=0
). Therefore
x=y\in\mathfrak{m}_{\theta}
, as desired.
∎

Corollary 4.3
.

Assume that
1/2\in R
if
\Phi_{\rho}\sim{}^{2}A_{n}\ (n\geq 3),{}^{2}D_{n}\ (n\geq 4)
or
{}^{2}E_{6}
, and that
1/3\in R
if
\Phi_{\rho}\sim{}^{3}D_{4}
. Then
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
is a normal subgroup of
G_{\sigma}(R)
.

Recall that in Section
2.3
, we defined the set
\text{Hom}_{1}(\Lambda_{\pi},R^{*})=\{\chi\in\text{Hom}(\Lambda_{\pi},R^{*})\mid\chi=\bar{\chi}_{\sigma}\}
. We also define
\text{Hom}_{1}(\Lambda_{\pi}/\Lambda_{r},R^{*})
as
\{\chi\in\text{Hom}_{1}(\Lambda_{\pi},R^{*})\mid\chi|_{\Lambda_{r}}=1\}
. The following theorem is a special case of the main theorem of
[
21
]
.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
G=G_{\sigma}(R)
be a twisted Chevalley group of type
\Phi_{\rho}\sim{}^{2}A_{n}\ (n\geq 3),{}^{2}D_{n}\ (n\geq 4)
or
{}^{2}E_{6}
and let
E=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
be its elementary subgroup. Assume that
1/2\in R
. Then
Z(G)=C_{G}(E)=\text{Hom}_{1}(\Lambda_{\pi}/\Lambda_{r},R^{*})
, where
Z(G)
is a centre of
G
and
C_{G}(E)
is a centralizer of
E
in
G
.

Remark
.

As before, we can state a similar result to Theorem
4.4
for the case of
\Phi_{\rho}\sim{}^{3}D_{4}
, assuming
1/3\in R
. The proof follows similar lines as those in
[
21
]
.

5.
Certain Subgroups of
G_{\sigma}(R)

Let
R
be a commutative ring with unity and let
\theta
be an automorphism of a ring
R
of order
2
or
3
. Let
J
be a
\theta
-invariant ideal of
R
(that is,
J
is an ideal of
R
such that
\theta(J)\subset J
). For
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}
, define

J_{[\alpha]}=\begin{cases}J_{\theta}&\text{if }[\alpha]\sim A_{1},\\
J&\text{if }[\alpha]\sim A_{1}^{2}\text{ or }A_{1}^{3},\\
\mathcal{A}(J)&\text{if }[\alpha]\sim A_{2};\\
\end{cases}

where
J_{\theta}=\{r\in J\mid r=\overline{r}\}=J\cap R_{\theta}
and
\mathcal{A}(J)=\{(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}(R)\mid a,b\in J\}.

The natural projection map
R\longrightarrow R/J
gives the canonical map
\phi:G_{\sigma}(R)\longrightarrow G_{\sigma}(R/J)
. Write
G_{\sigma}(J)=\ker{\phi}
and
G_{\sigma}(R,J)=\phi^{-1}(Z(G_{\sigma}(R/J))).
Let
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)
denote the subgroup of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)\cap G_{\sigma}(J)
generated by all
x_{[\alpha]}(t)
where
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}
and
t\in J_{[\alpha]}
. Let
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
be the normal subgroup of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
generated by
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)
. Note that
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
is also a subgroup of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)\cap G_{\sigma}(J)
as the later subgroup is normal in
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
and it contains
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)
.

Let
U_{\sigma}(J)
(resp.,
U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)
) be the subgroup of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
generated by
x_{[\alpha]}(t)
(resp.,
x_{-[\alpha]}(t)
) where
[\alpha]\in\Phi^{+}_{\rho}
and
t\in J_{[\alpha]}
. Define
T_{\sigma}(J)=G_{\sigma}(J)\cap T_{\sigma}(R),T_{\sigma}(R,J)=G_{\sigma}(R,J)\cap T_{\sigma}(R),H_{\sigma}(J)=E_{\sigma}(J)\cap T_{\sigma}(R),H_{\sigma}(R,J)=E_{\sigma}(R,J)\cap T_{\sigma}(R),H^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)\cap T_{\sigma}(R)
and
H^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\cap T_{\sigma}(R).

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
J
be any
\theta
-invariant ideal of
R
. Then each element of
U_{\sigma}(J)
is uniquely expressible in the form

x_{[\alpha_{1}]}(t_{1})\dots x_{[\alpha_{n}]}(t_{n})

where
[\alpha_{i}]\in\Phi^{+}_{\rho}
and
t_{i}\in J_{[\alpha_{i}]}
, the ordering of the roots is arbitrary chosen and fixed once for all.

Proof.

The proof is an easy consequence of the Chevalley commutator formula and is therefore omitted.
∎

Remark
.

We can state and prove a similar result for
U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)
.

Remark
.

Note that
U_{\sigma}(J)=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\cap U_{\sigma}(R)
and
U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\cap U^{-}_{\sigma}(R)
. This can be seen as follows: Clearly,
U_{\sigma}(J)\subset E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\cap U_{\sigma}(R)
. For the reverse inclusion, let
x\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\cap U_{\sigma}(R)
. Since
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\subset G_{\sigma}(J)
we have
x\equiv 1
(mod
J
). From the uniqueness in the above lemma, we conclude that
x\in U_{\sigma}(J)
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
J
be a
\theta
-invariant ideal of
R
contained in
rad(R)
, the Jacobson radical of
R
. Then for any
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho},t\in J_{[\alpha]},s\in R_{[\alpha]},
we have

x_{[\alpha]}(s)x_{-[\alpha]}(t)x_{[\alpha]}(s)^{-1}=x_{[\alpha]}(a)hx_{-[\alpha]}(b)

where
a,b\in J_{[\alpha]}
and
h\in H^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
.

Proof.

If
[\alpha]\sim A_{1},A_{1}^{2}
or
A_{1}^{3}
then for given
t\in J_{[\alpha]}
and
s\in R_{[\alpha]}
, we have
(1-st)\in R_{[\alpha]}^{*}
. In this case, we can take
a=-ts^{2}(1-st)^{-1},b=t(1-st)^{-1}
and
h=h_{[\alpha]}((1-st)^{-1})
. Clearly,
a,b\in J_{[\alpha]}
. But then
h\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
and hence
h\in H^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
.

If
[\alpha]\sim A_{2}
then for given
t=(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathcal{A}(J)
and
s=(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathcal{A}(R)
, we have
1-(s_{1}\bar{t_{1}}-\bar{s_{2}}t_{2})\in R^{*}.
Therefore, the equation

\begin{pmatrix}1&\bar{s}_{1}&s_{2}\\
0&1&s_{1}\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
t_{1}&1&0\\
t_{2}&\bar{t}_{1}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\bar{s_{1}}&\bar{s_{2}}\\
0&1&-s_{1}\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\bar{a_{1}}&a_{2}\\
0&1&a_{1}\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{u}&0&0\\
0&u\bar{u}^{-1}&0\\
0&0&u^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
b_{1}&1&0\\
b_{2}&\bar{b_{1}}&1\end{pmatrix}

has a solution for
u,a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}
and it is given by
u=(1-(\bar{t_{1}}s_{1}-t_{2}\bar{s_{2}}))^{-1},a_{1}=(t_{1}\bar{s_{2}}-\bar{t_{1}}{s_{1}}^{2}+t_{2}s_{1}\bar{s_{2}})(1-(\bar{t_{1}}s_{1}-t_{2}\bar{s_{2}}))^{-1},a_{2}=(t_{1}\bar{s_{1}}\bar{s_{2}}-\bar{t_{1}}s_{1}s_{2}+t_{2}s_{2}\bar{s_{2}})(1-(\bar{t_{1}}s_{1}-t_{2}\bar{s_{2}}))^{-1},b_{1}=(t_{1}-s_{1}\bar{t_{2}})(1-(t_{1}\bar{s_{1}}-\bar{t_{2}}{s_{2}}))^{-1},b_{2}=t_{2}(1-(\bar{t_{1}}s_{1}-t_{2}\bar{s_{2}}))^{-1}
. By simple calculation we can see that
(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})\in\mathcal{A}(J)
. But then

h_{[\alpha]}(u)=x_{[\alpha]}(a)^{-1}x_{[\alpha]}(s)x_{-[\alpha]}(t)x_{[\alpha]}(s)^{-1}x_{-[\alpha]}(b)^{-1}\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J).

Since,
h=h_{[\alpha]}(u)\in T_{\sigma}(R)
, we have
h=h_{[\alpha]}(u)\in H^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
.
∎

Let
[\alpha_{i}]
be the simple roots of
\Phi_{\rho}
. We define the height
ht([\alpha]):=\sum_{i=1}^{l}m_{i}
of a root
[\alpha]=\sum_{i=1}^{l}m_{i}[\alpha_{i}]
in
\Phi_{\rho}
. The order of the roots is
regular
if the height
ht([\alpha])
is an increasing function of
[\alpha]
. From now on we fix a regular ordering of the roots in
\Phi_{\rho}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
J
be any
\theta
-invariant ideal of
R
. Then for any
[\alpha],[\beta](\neq[\alpha])\in\Phi^{+}_{\rho}
and
t\in J_{[\alpha]},s\in R_{[\beta]},
we have

x_{-[\beta]}(s)x_{[\alpha]}(t)x_{-[\beta]}(s)^{-1}=xy

where
x\in U_{\sigma}(J)
and
y
is a product of
x_{-[\gamma]}(u)
’s (
u\in J_{[\gamma]}
) in
U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)
such that
-[\gamma]>-[\beta]
.

Proof.

Immediate from the Chevalley commutator formula for
[x_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1},x_{-[\beta]}(s)].

∎

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
J
be a
\theta
-invariant ideal of
R
contained in
rad(R)
. Then
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)=U_{\sigma}(J)H^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of
2.8
in
[
2
]
and
2.1
in
[
5
]
. However, for the convenience of the reader, we will provide the full proof here.

We write
U=U_{\sigma}(J),H=H^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
and
V=U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)
. Clearly,
UHV\subset E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
. For converge, it is enough to show that
UHV
is a normal subgroup of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
because then
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)\subset UHV
and hence
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\subset UHV
.

First, we will show that
UHV
is a subgroup of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
. In other words, we will show that
gh^{-1}\subset UHV
for every
g,h\in UHV
. For that, it is enough to show that
g(UHV)\subset UHV
for any element
g
of the form
x_{[\beta]}(t)\in U,h_{[\beta]}(t)\in H
and
x_{-[\beta]}(t)\in U^{-}
. If
g=x_{[\beta]}(t)
, then by Lemma
5.1
, we have
x_{[\beta]}(t)U\subset U
and hence
x_{[\beta]}(t)UHV\subset UHV
. Similarly, if
g=h_{[\beta]}(t)
, then from Proposition
3.14
, we have
h_{[\beta]}(t)U\subset UH
and hence
h_{[\beta]}(t)UHV\subset UHV
. Finally, if
g=x_{-[\beta]}(t),
we must show that

(1)

\displaystyle x_{-[\beta]}(t)U\subset UHV.

Because then
x_{-[\beta]}(t)UHV\subset(UHV)HV=UH(VH)V=UH(HV)V=UHV
, the second last equality is follows from Proposition
3.14
.

To prove (
1
), we must show that
x_{-[\beta]}(t)x\in UHV
for every
x\in U
. Write

x=x_{[\alpha_{1}]}(t_{1})\dots x_{[\alpha_{n}]}(t_{n})\in U,

where each
[\alpha_{i}]\in\Phi_{\rho}^{+}\ (i=1,\dots,n)
with
[\alpha_{1}]>\dots>[\alpha_{n}]
. Let
m=ht([\beta])
. We will use double induction on the pair
(m,n)
to prove our result. If
n=1
then for any pair
(m,1)
the result is follows from Lemma
5.2
, if
[\alpha_{1}]=[\beta]
; and from Lemma
5.3
, if
[\alpha_{1}]\neq[\beta]
. Assume that for all
1\leq k\leq n-1
, the result is true for the pairs
(m,k)
for all
m\geq 1
. We will show that it is also true for the pair
(m,n)
for all
m\geq 1
, that is, we will show that
x_{-[\beta]}(t)x_{[\alpha_{1}]}(t_{1})\dots x_{[\alpha_{n}]}(t_{n})\in UHV
.
If
[\beta]=[\alpha_{1}]
then, by Lemma
5.2
, we have
x_{-[\beta]}(t)x_{[\alpha_{1}]}(t_{1})=x_{[\alpha_{1}]}(t^{\prime}_{1})hx_{-[\beta]}(t^{\prime})
, where
h\in H
.
Thus, by induction hypothesis,

\displaystyle x_{-[\beta]}(t)x_{[\alpha_{1}]}(t_{1})\dots x_{[\alpha_{n}]}(t_{n})

\displaystyle=x_{[\alpha_{1}]}(t^{\prime}_{1})hx_{-[\beta]}(t^{\prime})x_{[\alpha_{2}]}(t_{2})\dots x_{[\alpha_{n}]}(t_{n})

\displaystyle\in UH(UHV)=U(HU)HV=U(UH)HV=UHV.

Similarly, if
[\beta]\neq[\alpha_{1}]
then, by Lemma
5.3
, we have
x_{-[\beta]}(t)x_{[\alpha_{1}]}(t_{1})=xyx_{-[\beta]}(t)
, where
x\in U
and
y\in V
as in Lemma
5.3
. Thus, by induction hypothesis,

x_{-[\beta]}(t)x_{[\alpha_{1}]}(t_{1})\dots x_{[\alpha_{n}]}(t_{n})=xyx_{-[\beta]}(t)x_{[\alpha_{2}]}(t_{2})\dots x_{[\alpha_{n}]}(t_{n})\in U(UHV)=UHV.

Now we will show that
UHV
is a normal subgroup of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
. It is suffices to show that
x_{\pm[\alpha]}(t)UHVx_{\pm[\alpha]}(t)^{-1}\subset UHV
for any root
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}^{+}
and
t\in R_{[\alpha]}
. Clearly,
x_{[\alpha]}(t)Ux_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1}\subset U
and
x_{[\alpha]}(t)Hx_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1}\subset UH
(see Proposition
3.14
). We will show that
x_{[\alpha]}(t)Vx_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1}\in UHV
. Let
y\in V
be such that
y=x_{-[\alpha_{1}]}(t_{1})\dots x_{-[\alpha_{n}]}(t_{n})
, where
[\alpha_{i}]\in\Phi^{+}_{\rho},t_{i}\in J_{[\alpha_{i}]}\ (i=1,\dots,n)
. Then

\displaystyle x_{[\alpha]}(t)yx_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1}

\displaystyle=x_{[\alpha]}(t)x_{[\alpha_{1}]}(t_{1})\dots x_{[\alpha_{n}]}(t_{n})x_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(x_{[\alpha]}(t)x_{[\alpha_{1}]}(t_{1})x_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1})\dots(x_{[\alpha]}(t)x_{[\alpha_{n}]}(t_{n}))x_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1})\in UHV.

The containment is follows because if
[\alpha_{i}]=[\alpha]
then, by Lemma
5.2
,
x_{[\alpha]}(t)x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(t_{i})x_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1}\in UHV
, and if
[\alpha_{i}]\neq[\alpha]
then, by Lemma
5.3
,
x_{[\alpha]}(t)x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(t_{i})x_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1}\in UV\subset UHV
, and also the fact that
UHV
is a subgroup of
G
. Finally, we have

\displaystyle x_{[\alpha]}(t)UHVx_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(x_{[\alpha]}(t)Ux_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1})(x_{[\alpha]}(t)Hx_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1})(x_{[\alpha]}(t)Vx_{[\alpha]}(t)^{-1})

\displaystyle\subset(U)(UH)(UHV)=U(HU)HV=U(UH)HV=UHV.

Similarly, one can show that
x_{-[\alpha]}(t)UHVx_{-[\alpha]}(t)^{-1}\subset UHV
.
∎

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
J
be a
\theta
-invariant ideal of
R
contained in
rad(R)
.
Set
P_{\sigma}(J)=U_{\sigma}(J)T_{\sigma}(R)U^{-}_{\sigma}(R)
and
Q_{\sigma}(J)=U_{\sigma}(R)T_{\sigma}(R)U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)
. Then
P_{\sigma}(J)
and
Q_{\sigma}(J)
are subgroups of
G_{\sigma}(R)
.

Proof.

Note that
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)=U_{\sigma}(J)H^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)
is normalized by
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
and
T_{\sigma}(R)
. Set
B_{\sigma}(R)=U_{\sigma}(R)T_{\sigma}(R)=T_{\sigma}(R)U_{\sigma}(R)
and
B_{\sigma}^{-}(R)=U_{\sigma}^{-}(R)T_{\sigma}(R)=T_{\sigma}(R)U_{\sigma}^{-}(R)
. Clearly, both
B_{\sigma}(R)
and
B^{-}_{\sigma}(R)
are subgroups of
G_{\sigma}(R).
By a similar argument as in Proposition
5.4
, we have

P_{\sigma}(J)=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)B^{-}_{\sigma}(R)=B^{-}_{\sigma}(R)E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\text{ and }Q_{\sigma}(J)=B_{\sigma}(R)E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)B_{\sigma}(R).

Therefore,
P_{\sigma}(J)
and
Q_{\sigma}(J)
are subgroups of
G_{\sigma}(R)
.
∎

For any
\theta
-invariant ideal
J
of
R
, we have the canonical map
\phi:G_{\sigma}(R)\longrightarrow G_{\sigma}(R/J)
as mentioned above. We now consider the canonical map
\phi^{\prime}:G(R)\longrightarrow G(R/J)
. Clearly,
\phi^{\prime}|_{G_{\sigma}(R)}=\phi
. Let
G(J)=\ker(\phi^{\prime})
and
G(R,J)=\phi^{\prime-1}(Z(G(R/J)))
, where
Z(G(R/J))
is the center of the group
G(R/J)
. Let
U(J)
(respectively,
U^{-}(J)
) be the subgroup of
G(R)
generated by
x_{\alpha}(t)
for
t\in J
and
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
(respectively,
\alpha\in\Phi^{-}
). Define
T(J)=G(J)\cap T(R)
and
T(R,J)=G(R,J)\cap T(R)
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
J
be any
\theta
-invariant ideal of
R
. Then

(1)

the subgroup
T_{\sigma}(J)
of
T_{\sigma}(R)
is generated by all
h(\chi)
such that
\chi=\bar{\chi}_{\sigma}
and
\chi(\mu)\equiv 1
(mod
J
) for every
\mu\in\Omega_{\pi}
, where
\Omega_{\pi}
is a set of all weights of the representation
\pi
.

(2)

the subgroup
T_{\sigma}(R,J)
of
T_{\sigma}(R)
is generated by all
h(\chi)
such that
\chi=\bar{\chi}_{\sigma}
and
\chi(\alpha)\equiv 1
(mod
J
) for every
\alpha\in\Phi
.

Proof.

To prove our result it is enough to prove that

(1)

T_{\sigma}(J)=T(J)\cap G_{\sigma}(R)
and
T(J)
is a subgroup of
T(R)
generated by all
h(\chi)
such that
\chi(\mu)\equiv 1
(mod
J
) for every
\mu\in\Omega_{\pi}
.

(2)

T_{\sigma}(R,J)=T(R,J)\cap G_{\sigma}(R)
and
T(R,J)
is a subgroup of
T(R)
generated by all
h(\chi)
such that
\chi(\alpha)\equiv 1
(mod
J
) for every
\alpha\in\Phi
.

Since
G_{\sigma}(J)=G(J)\cap G_{\sigma}(R)
and
T_{\sigma}(R)=T(R)\cap G_{\sigma}(R)
, the first assertion of part (a) is clear. The second assertion of part (a) directly follows from the definition of
T(J)
and the action of
h(\chi)
on the weight spaces corresponding to the representation
\pi
(see
2.1
).

Note that the center of
G(R/J)
is
Z(G(R/J))=\text{Hom }(\Lambda_{\pi}/\Lambda_{r},(R/J)^{*})
(see
[
4
]
) and the center of
G_{\sigma}(R/J)
is
Z(G_{\sigma}(R/J))=\text{Hom}_{1}(\Lambda_{\pi}/\Lambda_{r},(R/J)^{*})
(see Theorem
4.4
). Therefore,
Z(G_{\sigma}(R/J))=Z(G(R/J))\cap G_{\sigma}(R/J)
. But then
G_{\sigma}(R,J)=G(R,J)\cap G_{\sigma}(R)
and hence the first assertion of part (b) follows. For the second assertion of part (b), let
T^{\prime}(R,J)
be the subgroup of
T(R)
generated by all
h(\chi)
such that
\chi(\alpha)\equiv 1
(mod
J
), for every
\alpha\in\Phi
. We want to show that
T^{\prime}(R,J)=T(R,J)
.
Let
h(\chi)\in T(R,J)=G(R,J)\cap T(R)
. Since
G(R,J)
is normal subgroup of
G(R)
, we have
[h(\chi),x_{\alpha}(1)]\in G(R,J)
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
, that is
x_{\alpha}(\chi(\alpha)-1)\in G(R,J)
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
. Hence, by the main theorem of
[
4
]
,
\chi(\alpha)\equiv 1
(mod
J
) for all
\alpha\in\Phi
. Thus,
T(R,J)\subset T^{\prime}(R,J)
. For the reverse inclusion, let
h(\chi)\in T^{\prime}(R,J)
. Then
\chi(\alpha)\equiv 1
(mod
J
), for every
\alpha\in\Phi
and hence
\phi^{\prime}(h(\chi))\in Z(G(R/J))
(again by the main theorem of
[
4
]
). That is,
h(\chi)\in G(R,J)
. Thus, we have
T^{\prime}(R,J)\subset T(R,J)
.
∎

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
J
be a
\theta
-invariant ideal of
R
contained in
rad(R)
. Then

G_{\sigma}(J)=U_{\sigma}(J)T_{\sigma}(J)U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)\subset G^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R).

Proof.

By
2.3
of
[
5
]
, we have
G(J)=U(J)T(J)U^{-}(J)
. Note that
G_{\sigma}(J)=G(J)\cap G_{\sigma}(R)
, that is,
G_{\sigma}(J)=\{U(J)T(J)U^{-}(J)\}\cap G_{\sigma}(R)
. Also, we have
U_{\sigma}(J)=\{x\in U(J)\mid\sigma(x)=x\},U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)=\{x\in U^{-}(J)\mid\sigma(x)=x\}
and
T_{\sigma}(J)=T_{\sigma}(R)\cap G_{\sigma}(J)=T(J)\cap G_{\sigma}(R)
(by the proof of Lemma
5.6
). Since
U_{\sigma}(J)\cap U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)=U_{\sigma}(J)\cap T_{\sigma}(J)=U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)\cap T_{\sigma}(J)=\{1\}
, we have

G_{\sigma}(J)=U_{\sigma}(J)T_{\sigma}(J)U^{-}_{\sigma}(J).

It is also clear that
G_{\sigma}(J)\subset G^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
.
∎

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
R
be a semi-local ring. Then
G_{\sigma}(R)=G^{0}_{\sigma}(R)=G^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
.

Proof.

Let
J=rad(R)
. Since
R
is semi-local, it has finitely many maximal ideals, say
\mathfrak{m}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{m}_{k}
. Therefore, by the Chinese remainder theorem, we have
R/J=\prod_{i=1}^{k}R/\mathfrak{m}_{i}
.
Write
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}=\theta(\mathfrak{m}_{i})
. Set

J_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{m}_{i}&\text{ if }\mathfrak{m}_{i}=\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{i},\\
\mathfrak{m}_{i}\cap\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}&\text{ if }\mathfrak{m}_{i}\neq\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}\text{ and }o(\theta)=2,\\
\mathfrak{m}_{i}\cap\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}\cap\bar{\bar{\mathfrak{m}}}_{i}&\text{ if }\mathfrak{m}_{i}\neq\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}\text{ and }o(\theta)=3.\end{cases}

By the proof of Proposition
2.2
of
[
20
]
, we have
G_{\sigma}(R/J_{i})=G^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R/J_{i})
(that proof only addresses the case where
o(\theta)=2
. However, the proof for the case where
o(\theta)=3
follows a similar structure).

Since
R/J=\prod_{i=1}^{k}R/\mathfrak{m}_{i}=\prod_{i=1}^{l}R/J_{i}
, we have
G_{\sigma}(R/J)=\prod_{i=1}^{l}G_{\sigma}(R/J_{i}).
But then

G_{\sigma}(R/J)=\prod_{i=1}^{l}G_{\sigma}(R/J_{i})=\prod_{i=1}^{l}G^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R/J_{i})=G^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R/J).

On the other hand, from Proposition
5.7
,
G_{\sigma}(J)\subset G^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
. Therefore
G_{\sigma}(R)\subset G^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
. Hence
G^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)=G^{0}_{\sigma}(R)=G_{\sigma}(R)
, as desired.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
R
be a semi-local ring and let
J
be a
\theta
-invariant ideal of
R
contained in
rad(R)
. Then

G_{\sigma}(R,J)=U_{\sigma}(J)T_{\sigma}(R,J)U^{-}_{\sigma}(J).

Proof.

Since
G_{\sigma}(J)
is normalized by
T_{\sigma}(R,J)
, we conclude that
G_{\sigma}(J)T_{\sigma}(R,J)
is a subgroup of
G_{\sigma}(R,J)
. On the other hand, by the above proposition, we have
G_{\sigma}(R)=G^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)T_{\sigma}(R)
. Let
z\in G_{\sigma}(R,J)\subset G_{\sigma}(R)
. Then there exist
x\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
and
y\in T_{\sigma}(R)
such that
z=xy
. Now consider the canonical map
\phi:G_{\sigma}(R)\longrightarrow G_{\sigma}(R/J)
. Then
\phi(z)=\phi(x)\phi(y)\in Z(G_{\sigma}(R/J))
. Since
\phi(y)\in T_{\sigma}(R/J)
, we obtain
\phi(x)\in T_{\sigma}(R/J)
. To be precious,
\phi(x)\in H^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R/J)
as
x\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
. But then there exist
h\in H^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
such that
\phi(h)=\phi(x)
, that is,
xh^{-1}\in G_{\sigma}(J)
. Hence
hy\in T_{\sigma}(R,J)
. Write
x^{\prime}=xh^{-1}
and
y^{\prime}=hy
. Then
z=xy=x^{\prime}y^{\prime}\in G_{\sigma}(J)T_{\sigma}(R,J)
. Therefore,
G_{\sigma}(R,J)=G_{\sigma}(J)T_{\sigma}(R,J)=U_{\sigma}(J)T_{\sigma}(R,J)U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)
, the last equality is due to Proposition
5.7
.
∎

6.
The Subgroup
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)

In this section, we will explore several important properties of the subgroup
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
. Similar properties have been studied by L. N. Vaserstein in
[
23
]
for the case of Chevalley groups. Using his ideas, we will state and prove analogous properties for twisted Chevalley groups. For the remainder of this paper, we adopt the following conventions.

Convention
.

Assume that
\Phi_{\rho}
is irreducible and the rank of
\Phi_{\rho}>1
. Any ideal
J
of
R
is
\theta
-invariant. If
o(\theta)=2
then assume that
1/2\in R
and if
o(\theta)=3
then assume that
1/2\in R
and
1/3\in R
.

Proposition 6.1
.

For any ideal
J
of
R
, the subgroup
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
of
G_{\sigma}(R)
is normal. In other words,

[G_{\sigma}(R),E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)]\subset E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J).

Proof.

First, consider the case where
J=R
. Then, by definition,
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
. The result in this case follows from the fact that
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
is a normal subgroup of
G_{\sigma}(R)
(see Corollary
4.3
).

Now suppose
J\subsetneq R
. Let
h\in G_{\sigma}(R)
and
g\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
.
We want to prove that
hgh^{-1}\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
.
We consider the ring
R^{\prime}:=\{(r,s)\in R\times R\mid r-s\in J\}
and its ideal
J^{\prime}:=\{(r,0)\in R\times R\mid r\in J\}
.
The automorphism
\theta
of the ring
R
can be naturally induced to an automorphism of
R^{\prime}
, and we denote it by the same letter
\theta
.
Therefore, the group
G_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})
makes sense.
Consider an element
h^{\prime\prime}:=(h,h)
of the group
G_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})\subset G_{\sigma}(R)\times G_{\sigma}(R).

Observe that
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
is embedded into the group
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime},J^{\prime})
by
x\mapsto x^{\prime}:=(x,1).
(This can be seen as follows: There is a natural embedding from
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)
into
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J^{\prime})
given by
x\mapsto(x,1)
. Now any
y\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
is can be written as a product of the form
\prod_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}x_{i}g_{i}^{-1}
, where
x_{i}\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)
and
g_{i}\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
. But then
(y,1)=(\prod_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}x_{i}g_{i}^{-1},1)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(g_{i},g_{i})(x_{i},1)(g_{i},g_{i})^{-1}\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime},J^{\prime})
, as desired.)

Now we claim that
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime},J^{\prime})=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})\cap G_{\sigma}(J^{\prime})
. Clearly, by definition,
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime},J^{\prime})\subset E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})\cap G_{\sigma}(J^{\prime})
. For converse, let

x=\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(t_{i})\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})\cap G_{\sigma}(J^{\prime})

where
t_{i}\in R^{\prime}_{[\alpha_{i}]}
.
For each
t_{i}\in R^{\prime}_{[\alpha_{i}]}
, choose elements
s_{i}\in R^{\prime}_{[\alpha_{i}]}
and
u_{i}\in J^{\prime}_{[\alpha_{i}]}
as follows:

(1)

If
[\alpha_{i}]\sim A_{1}
,
A_{1}^{2}
, or
A_{1}^{3}
and
t_{i}=(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})
, then set
s_{i}=(\beta_{i},\beta_{i})
and
u_{i}=(\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i},0)
. It is clear that
t_{i}=s_{i}+u_{i}
.

(2)

If
[\alpha_{i}]\sim A_{2},

t_{i}=(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})\in\mathcal{A}(R^{\prime})
,
\alpha_{i}=(a_{1},a_{2})\in R^{\prime}
and
\beta_{i}=(b_{1},b_{2})\in R^{\prime}
, then set
s_{i}=(\gamma_{i},\delta_{i})\in\mathcal{A}(R^{\prime})
and
u_{i}=(\mu_{i},\nu_{i})\in\mathcal{A}(R^{\prime})
, where
\gamma_{i}=(a_{2},a_{2})\in R^{\prime},\delta_{i}=(b_{2},b_{2})\in R^{\prime},\mu_{i}=(a_{1}-a_{2},0)\in R^{\prime}
and
\nu_{i}=(b_{1}-b_{2}-\overline{a_{2}}(a_{1}-a_{2}),0)\in R^{\prime}
. Clearly,
t_{i}=s_{i}\oplus u_{i}
.

Set

y_{k}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(s_{i})\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})

for
0\leq k\leq n.
Clearly,
y_{0}=1
(by the definition).
We claim that
y_{n}=1
. Since
x\in G_{\sigma}(J^{\prime})
, we have
x\equiv 1
(mod
J^{\prime}
). But then
y_{n}\equiv 1
(mod
J^{\prime}
), that is,
\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(s_{i}+J^{\prime})=1
in
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime}/J^{\prime})
(the notion of
s_{i}+J^{\prime}
is clear even when
[\alpha]\sim A_{2}
). Note that there is a natural embedding from
R^{\prime}/J^{\prime}
to
R/J\times R
which induces an embedding from the group
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime}/J^{\prime})
to the group
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R/J\times R)\cong E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R/J)\times E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
.
Under this embedding,

\prod_{i=1}^{n}(x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(\beta_{i}+J),x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(\beta_{i}))=(\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(\beta_{i}+J),\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(\beta_{i}))=(1,1)

in
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R/J)\times E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
. In particular,
\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(\beta_{i})=1
in
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
. Thus

\displaystyle y_{n}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(s_{i})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(\beta_{i},\beta_{i})=(\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(\beta_{i}),\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(\beta_{i}))=(1,1)=1.

This proves our claim. Finally,

x=\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(s_{i})x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(u_{i})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}y_{i-1}^{-1}y_{i}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(u_{i})=y_{0}^{-1}\Bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(u_{i})y_{i}^{-1}\Bigg)y_{n}\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime},J^{\prime}),

as desired.

Again by Corollary
4.3
,
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})
is a normal subgroup of
G_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})
. So
h^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime}(h^{\prime\prime})^{-1}\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime}),
where
g^{\prime}=(g,1)\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})
. On the other hand,
h^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime}(h^{\prime\prime})^{-1}=(hgh^{-1},1)\in G_{\sigma}(J^{\prime})
. Therefore
h^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime}(h^{\prime\prime})^{-1}\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})\cap G_{\sigma}(J^{\prime})=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime},J^{\prime}),
hence
hgh^{-1}\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
. Thus
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
is normal in
G_{\sigma}(R)
.
∎

We derive the following corollary from the proof of the above Proposition.

Corollary 6.2
.

[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),G_{\sigma}(J)]\subset E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
.

Proof.

Take any
h\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
and
g\in G_{\sigma}(J)
. Define, as in proof of Proposition
6.1
,
h^{\prime\prime}=(h,h)\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})
and
g^{\prime}=(g,1)\in G_{\sigma}(J^{\prime})
. Then
[h^{\prime\prime},g^{\prime}]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})\cap G_{\sigma}(J^{\prime})=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime},J^{\prime})
(as
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})
and
G_{\sigma}(J^{\prime})
are normal subgroups of
G_{\sigma}(R^{\prime})
). Since
[h^{\prime\prime},g^{\prime}]=([h,g],1)
, we have
[h,g]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J).
Thus,
[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),G_{\sigma}(J)]\subset E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
.
∎

Proposition 6.3
.

For any ideal
J
of
R
, the subgroup
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
is generated by elements of the form
x_{[\alpha]}(r)x_{-[\alpha]}(u)x_{[\alpha]}(r)^{-1}
with
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho},r\in R_{[\alpha]}
and
u\in J_{[\alpha]}
.

Proof.

Let
H
be the subgroup of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
generated by all
x_{[\alpha]}(r)x_{-[\alpha]}(u)x_{[\alpha]}(r)^{-1}
, where
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho},r\in R_{[\alpha]},
and
u\in J_{[\alpha]}
. We aim to prove that
H=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
. Since
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)\subset H
, it suffices to show that
H
is a normal subgroup of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
. To demonstrate this, we need to verify that

g=x_{[\beta]}(s)x_{[\alpha]}(r)x_{-[\alpha]}(u)x_{[\alpha]}(r)^{-1}x_{[\beta]}(s)^{-1}\in H

for all
[\alpha],[\beta]\in\Phi_{\rho},r\in R_{[\alpha]},s\in R_{[\beta]}
, and
u\in J_{[\alpha]}
.

Case A.
[\alpha]\neq\pm[\beta]
.
For
[\gamma],[\delta](\neq-[\gamma])\in\Phi_{\rho}
, we have

[x_{[\gamma]}(a),x_{[\delta]}(b)]=\prod x_{i[\gamma]+j[\delta]}(f_{i,j}(a,b)),

where
f_{i,j}
is function of
a
and
b
with the property that
f_{i,j}(a,b)\in J_{i[\gamma]+j[\delta]}
if
a\in J_{[\gamma]}
or
b\in J_{[\delta]}
. Since no convex combination of the roots
-[\alpha],[\beta]
and
i[\alpha]+j[\beta]\ (i,j\neq 0)
is
0
, we have

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=x_{[\beta]}(s)x_{[\alpha]}(r)x_{-[\alpha]}(u)x_{[\alpha]}(r)^{-1}x_{[\beta]}(s)^{-1}

\displaystyle=x_{[\alpha]}(r)x_{-[\alpha]}(u)x_{[\alpha]}(r)^{-1}\Big(\prod x_{i[\alpha]+j[\beta]}(h_{i,j}(s,t,u))\Big)\in H,

where
h_{i,j}
is function of
s,t
and
u
such that
h_{i,j}(s,t,u)\in J_{i[\alpha]+j[\beta]}
.

Case B.
[\alpha]=\pm[\beta]
.
Note that, if
[\alpha]=[\beta]
then there is nothing to prove. Now assume that
[\alpha]=-[\beta]
. We then have

g=x_{-[\alpha]}(s)x_{[\alpha]}(r)x_{-[\alpha]}(u)x_{[\alpha]}(r)^{-1}x_{-[\alpha]}(s)^{-1}.

Since the rank of
\Phi_{\rho}>1
, there exists
[\gamma]\in\Phi_{\rho}
such that the subroot system
\Phi^{\prime}
generated by
[\alpha]
and
[\gamma]
is connected of rank
2
. WLOG, we can assume that
[\alpha],[\gamma]
is base of
\Phi^{\prime}
. Set
\Phi^{\prime}_{+}
be the set of positive roots of
\Phi^{\prime}
with respect to this base,
\Phi^{\prime}_{-}=-\Phi^{\prime}_{+},\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{+}=\{i[\alpha]+j[\gamma]\in\Phi^{\prime}_{+}\mid j>0\},
and
\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{-}=-\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{+}
. Write
U^{\prime\prime}_{+}(J)
(resp.,
U^{\prime\prime}_{-}(J)
) for the subgroup of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
generated by
x_{[\delta]}(t)
with
[\delta]\in\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{+}
(resp.,
[\delta]\in\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{-}
) and
t\in J_{[\delta]}
. Then
U^{\prime\prime}_{+}(J)
and
U^{\prime\prime}_{-}(J)
are subgroups of
H
.

Now, by Lemma
6.4
(below), for given
u\in J_{[\alpha]}
we can find
u_{1}\in J_{[\alpha]+[\gamma]}
and
u_{2}\in R_{[\gamma]}
such that

x_{-[\alpha]}(u)=[x_{-([\alpha]+[\gamma])}(u_{1}),x_{[\gamma]}(u_{2})]h^{\prime}

with
h^{\prime}\in U^{\prime\prime}_{-}(J)
. Set

\displaystyle g_{1}

\displaystyle:=x_{-[\alpha]}(s)x_{[\alpha]}(r)x_{-([\alpha]+[\gamma])}(u_{1})x_{[\alpha]}(r)^{-1}x_{-[\alpha]}(s)^{-1}\in U^{\prime\prime}_{-}(J),

\displaystyle g_{2}

\displaystyle:=x_{-[\alpha]}(s)x_{[\alpha]}(r)x_{[\gamma]}(u_{2})x_{[\alpha]}(r)^{-1}x_{-[\alpha]}(s)^{-1}\in U^{\prime\prime}_{+}(R),

\displaystyle g_{3}

\displaystyle:=x_{-[\alpha]}(s)x_{[\alpha]}(r)h^{\prime}x_{[\alpha]}(r)^{-1}x_{-[\alpha]}(s)^{-1}\in U^{\prime\prime}_{-}(J).

Then
g=[g_{1},g_{2}]g_{3}
, which contained in
H
by Lemma
6.5
(below).
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

For given
u\in J_{[\alpha]}
we can find
u_{1}\in J_{[\alpha]+[\gamma]}
and
u_{2}\in R_{[\gamma]}
such that

x_{-[\alpha]}(u)=[x_{-([\alpha]+[\gamma])}(u_{1}),x_{[\gamma]}(u_{2})]h^{\prime}

with
h^{\prime}\in U^{\prime\prime}_{-}(J)
.

Proof.

The Chevalley commutator formula for
[x_{-([\alpha]+[\gamma])}(u_{1}),x_{[\gamma]}(u_{2})]
suggests that depending on the types of the pair of roots
(-[\alpha]-[\gamma],[\gamma])
we can choose
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
as follows:

Type of pair
(-[\alpha]-[\gamma],[\gamma])

u_{1}

u_{2}

(b-i)

u

\pm 1

(b-ii)

u
or
\bar{u}

\pm 1

(c-i)

u

\pm 1/2

(c-ii)

(u,u\bar{u}/2)
or
(\bar{u},u\bar{u}/2)

(\pm 1,1/2)

(d-i)

u

\pm 1

(d-ii)

(a,b):=u
or
(\bar{a},b)
or
(a,\bar{b})

\pm 1

(e)

u

\pm 1

(g)

u

\pm 1/3

Note that each
u_{1}\in J_{[\alpha]+[\gamma]}
and
u_{2}\in R_{[\gamma]}
. An immediate observation from Chevalley commutator formula for
[x_{-([\alpha]+[\gamma])}(u_{1}),x_{[\gamma]}(u_{2})]
is that
h^{\prime}\in U^{\prime\prime}_{-}(J)
.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

[U^{\prime\prime}_{-}(J),U^{\prime\prime}_{+}(R)]\subset H
.

Proof.

Let
h\in U^{\prime\prime}_{-}(J)
and
g\in U^{\prime\prime}_{+}(R)
. Write

h=x_{-[\alpha_{1}]}(u_{1})\dots x_{-[\alpha_{n}]}(u_{n}),

where
[\alpha_{i}]\in\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{+}
and
0\neq u_{i}\in J_{[\alpha_{i}]}
. We want to show that
[h,g]\in H.
For that we use induction of
n
. If
n=1
then
[h,g]=x_{-[\alpha_{1}]}(u_{1})gx_{-[\alpha_{1}]}(u_{1})^{-1}g^{-1}.
Write
g=x_{[\beta_{1}]}(v_{1})\dots x_{[\beta_{m}]}(v_{m})
where
[\beta_{i}]\in\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{+}
and
0\neq v_{i}\in R_{[\beta_{i}]}
. If
[\beta_{i}]\neq-[\alpha_{1}]
for every
i=1,\dots,m
, then we are done by Chevalley commutator relations. If there is some
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
such that
[\beta_{i}]=[\alpha_{1}]
then also we are done by the definition of
H
and Chevalley commutator relations.

Now for general
n
,

\displaystyle[h,g]

\displaystyle=hgh^{-1}g^{-1}

\displaystyle=x_{-[\alpha_{1}]}(u_{1})[x_{-[\alpha_{2}]}(u_{2})\dots x_{-[\alpha_{n}]}(u_{n}),g][g,x_{-[\alpha_{1}]}(u_{1})^{-1}]x_{-[\alpha_{1}]}(u_{1})^{-1}

\displaystyle\in H.

Which proves the lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
:

Note that
[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)]\subset[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),G_{\sigma}(R,J)]
and
[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)]\subset[G_{\sigma}(R),E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)]
.
Also, by Proposition
6.1
, we have
[G_{\sigma}(R),E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)]\subset E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
.
Therefore, to prove our proposition, it is enough to prove the following:

(1)

E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\subset[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)].

(2)

[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),G_{\sigma}(R,J)]\subset E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J).

Since
H:=[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)]
is normal in
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
, to prove (i) it is enough to prove that
x_{[\alpha]}(u)\in H
for every
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}
and
u\in J_{[\alpha]}
. As in the proof of Proposition
6.3
, since the rank of
\Phi_{\rho}>1
, there exists
[\beta]\in\Phi_{\rho}
such that the subsystem
\Phi^{\prime}
generated by
[\alpha]
and
[\beta]
is connected of rank
2
. WLOG, we can assume that
[\alpha],[\beta]
is base of
\Phi^{\prime}
.

Case A.
\Phi^{\prime}\sim A_{2}
.
In this case, the pair of roots
[\alpha]+[\beta]
and
-[\beta]
are of type
(b)
.

[\alpha]
[\beta]
[\alpha]+[\beta]
-[\alpha]
-[\beta]
-[\alpha]-[\beta]

(b-i)

If
[\alpha]+[\beta]\sim A_{1}
and
-[\beta]\sim A_{1}
, then so is
[\alpha]=([\alpha]+[\beta])+(-[\beta])
. In this case, for given
u\in J_{[\alpha]}=J_{\theta}
we have

x_{[\alpha]}(u)=[x_{[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm 1),x_{-[\beta]}(u)]\in H.

(b-ii)

If
[\alpha]+[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
-[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{2}
, then so is
[\alpha]=([\alpha]+[\beta])+(-[\beta])
. In this case, for given
u\in J_{[\alpha]}=J
we write
u^{\prime}=u
or
\bar{u}\in J
. Then we have

x_{[\alpha]}(u)=[x_{[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm 1),x_{-[\beta]}(u^{\prime})]\in H.

Case B.
\Phi^{\prime}\sim B_{2}
and
[\alpha]
is a long root.
In this case, the pair of roots
[\alpha]+[\beta]
and
-[\beta]
are of type
(c)
.

[\alpha]
[\beta]
[\alpha]+[\beta]
[\alpha]+2[\beta]
-[\alpha]
-[\beta]
-[\alpha]-[\beta]
-
[\alpha]-2[\beta]

(c-i)

If
[\alpha]+[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
-[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{2}
, then
[\alpha]=([\alpha]+[\beta])+(-[\beta])\sim A_{1}
. In this case, for given
u\in J_{[\alpha]}=J_{\theta}
we have

x_{[\alpha]}(u)=[x_{[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm 1/2),x_{-[\beta]}(u)]\in H.

(c-ii)

If
[\alpha]+[\beta]\sim A_{2}
and
-[\beta]\sim A_{2}
, then
[\alpha]=([\alpha]+[\beta])+(-[\beta])\sim A_{1}^{2}
. In this case, for given
u\in J_{[\alpha]}=J
we write
u^{\prime}=u
or
\bar{u}\in J
. Then we have

x_{[\alpha]}(u)=[x_{[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm 1,1/2),x_{-[\beta]}(u^{\prime},u\bar{u}/2)]\in H.

Case C.
\Phi^{\prime}\sim B_{2}
and
[\alpha]
is a short root.
In this case, the pair of roots
[\alpha]+[\beta]
and
-[\beta]
are of type
(d)
with
[\alpha]+[\beta]
being the short root.

[\alpha]
[\beta]
2[\alpha]+[\beta]
[\alpha]+[\beta]
-[\alpha]
-[\beta]
-2[\alpha]-[\beta]
-[\alpha]-[\beta]

(d-i)

If
[\alpha]+[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
-[\beta]\sim A_{1}
, then
[\alpha]=([\alpha]+[\beta])+(-[\beta])\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
2[\alpha]+[\beta]=2([\alpha]+[\beta])+(-[\beta])\sim A_{1}
. In this case, for given
u\in J_{[\alpha]}=J
we have

\displaystyle x_{[\alpha]}(u)x_{2[\alpha]+[\beta]}(u)

\displaystyle=[x_{-[\beta]}(\pm u),x_{[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm 1)]

\displaystyle=[x_{[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm 1),x_{-[\beta]}(\pm u)]^{-1}\in H.

Now observe that
[\alpha]\sim A_{1}^{2},[\alpha]+[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
2[\alpha]+[\beta]\sim A_{1}
. Then by similar argument as in
(c-i)
above, we can conclude that
x_{2[\alpha]+[\beta]}(u)\in H
. Hence

x_{[\alpha]}(u)=(x_{[\alpha]}(u)x_{2[\alpha]+[\beta]}(u))(x_{2[\alpha]+[\beta]}(u))^{-1}\in H.

(d-ii)

If
[\alpha]+[\beta]\sim A_{2}
and
-[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{2}
, then
[\alpha]=([\alpha]+[\beta])+(-[\beta])\sim A_{2}
and
2[\alpha]+[\beta]=2([\alpha]+[\beta])+(-[\beta])\sim A_{1}^{2}
. In this case, for given
u=(u_{1},u_{2})\in J_{[\alpha]}=\mathcal{A}(J)=\mathcal{J}
we have

\displaystyle x_{[\alpha]}(u_{1},u_{2})x_{2[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm u_{2})

\displaystyle=[x_{-[\beta]}(\pm 1),x_{[\alpha]+[\beta]}(u_{1},u_{2})]

\displaystyle=[x_{[\alpha]+[\beta]}(u_{1},u_{2}),x_{-[\beta]}(\pm 1)]^{-1}\in H.

Now observe that
[\alpha]\sim A_{2},[\alpha]+[\beta]\sim A_{2}
and
2[\alpha]+[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{2}
. Then by similar argument as in
(c-ii)
above, we can conclude that
x_{2[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm u_{2})\in H
. Hence

x_{[\alpha]}(u_{1},u_{2})=(x_{[\alpha]}(u_{1},u_{2})x_{2[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm u_{2}))(x_{2[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm u_{2}))^{-1}\in H.

Case D.
\Phi^{\prime}\sim G_{2}
and
[\alpha]
is a long root.
In this case, we consider a subroot system
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
of
\Phi^{\prime}
generated by roots
[\alpha]
and
[\alpha]+3[\beta]
. Note that
\Phi^{\prime\prime}\sim A_{2}
and hence, by case 1 (replace
[\beta]
by
[\alpha]+3[\beta]
), we can conclude that
x_{[\alpha]}(u)\in H
.

[\alpha]
[\beta]
[\alpha]+[\beta]
2[\alpha]+3[\beta]
[\alpha]+2[\beta]
[\alpha]+3[\beta]
-[\alpha]
-[\beta]
-[\alpha]-[\beta]
-2[\alpha]-3[\beta]
-[\alpha]-2[\beta]
-[\alpha]-3[\beta]

Case E.
\Phi^{\prime}\sim G_{2}
and
[\alpha]
is a short root.
In this case, the pair of roots
2[\alpha]+[\beta]
and
-[\alpha]-[\beta]
are of type
(f)
.

[\alpha]
[\beta]
[\alpha]+[\beta]
2[\alpha]+[\beta]
3[\alpha]+[\beta]
3[\alpha]+2[\beta]
-[\alpha]
-[\beta]
-[\alpha]-[\beta]
-2[\alpha]-[\beta]
-3[\alpha]-[\beta]
-3[\alpha]-2[\beta]

Observe that
[\alpha],2[\alpha]+[\beta],-[\alpha]-[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{3}
and
-[\beta],3[\alpha]+[\beta]\sim A_{1}
. For given
u\in J_{[\alpha]}=J
we write
(u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime})=(\bar{u},\bar{\bar{u}})
or
(\bar{\bar{u}},\bar{u})
. Then we have

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptx_{[\alpha]}(u)x_{3[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm(u^{2}+(u^{\prime})^{2}+(u^{\prime\prime})^{2}-2uu^{\prime}-2u^{\prime}u^{\prime\prime}-2uu^{\prime\prime})/4)x_{-[\beta]}(\pm(u+u^{\prime}+u^{\prime\prime})/2)

\displaystyle=[x_{2[\alpha]+[\beta]}((u+u^{\prime}-u^{\prime\prime})/2),x_{-[\alpha]-[\beta]}(\pm 1)]\in H.

Now observe that
-[\beta]\sim A_{1}
and
3[\alpha]+[\beta]\sim A_{1}
. Then by similar argument as in Case A above, we can conclude that
x_{3[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm(u^{2}+(u^{\prime})^{2}+(u^{\prime\prime})^{2}-2uu^{\prime}-2u^{\prime}u^{\prime\prime}-2uu^{\prime\prime})/4)\in H
and
x_{-[\beta]}(\pm(u+u^{\prime}+u^{\prime\prime})^{2}/4)\in H
. Hence

\displaystyle x_{[\alpha]}(u)

\displaystyle=(x_{[\alpha]}(u)x_{3[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm(u^{2}+(u^{\prime})^{2}+(u^{\prime\prime})^{2}-2uu^{\prime}-2u^{\prime}u^{\prime\prime}-2uu^{\prime\prime})/4)

\displaystyle\hskip 19.91692ptx_{-[\beta]}(\pm(u+u^{\prime}+u^{\prime\prime})/2))(x_{3[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\pm(u^{2}+(u^{\prime})^{2}+(u^{\prime\prime})^{2}-2uu^{\prime}-2u^{\prime}u^{\prime\prime}-2uu^{\prime\prime})/4)

\displaystyle\hskip 39.83385ptx_{-[\beta]}(\pm(u+u^{\prime}+u^{\prime\prime})/2))^{-1}\in H.

This proves part (i). Now for part (ii), we consider the groups
M:=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
and
N:=(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)\cap G_{\sigma}(J))/E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
. Observe that the group
M
is perfect (put
J=R
in part (i)) and the group
N
is commutative (by Corollary
6.2
). For a fixed
g\in G_{\sigma}(R,J)
, define a map
\psi_{g}:M\longrightarrow N
given by
h\longmapsto[h,g]E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
. Then
\psi_{g}
is a well-defined homomorphism from the perfect group
M
to a commutative group
N
. Hence
\psi_{g}
must be trivial, i.e.,
[h,g]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
for all
h\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R).
Thus,
[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),G_{\sigma}(R,J)]\subset E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J),
as desired. ∎

Corollary 6.6
.

The group
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
is perfect, that is,
[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)]=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
.

Proof.

Immediate by putting
J=R
in the above proposition.
∎

Corollary 6.7
.

Every subgroup of
G_{\sigma}(R,J)
containing
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
is normalized by
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
.

Proof.

Let
H
be a subgroup of
G_{\sigma}(R,J)
containing
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
. Then

[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(J)]\subset[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),H]\subset[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),G_{\sigma}(R,J)].

By Theorem
1.3
, we have
[E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R),H]=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\subset H
. Therefore
H
is normalized by
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
.
∎

Corollary 6.8
.

Let
C_{\sigma}(R,J)=\{x\in G_{\sigma}(R)\mid[x,E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)]\subset E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\}
. Then
G_{\sigma}(R,J)=C_{\sigma}(R,J)
.

Proof.

Clearly, by Theorem
1.3
,
G_{\sigma}(R,J)\subset C_{\sigma}(R,J)
. By definition, we have
G_{\sigma}(R,J)=\{x\in G_{\sigma}(R)\mid[x,G_{\sigma}(R)]\subset G_{\sigma}(J)\}
. Let
Z(G)
be the centre of
G=G_{\sigma}(R/J)
and
C_{G}(E)
the centralizer of
E=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R/J)
in
G
. Then, by Theorem
4.4
, we have
Z(G)=C_{G}(E)
. But then

G_{\sigma}(R,J)=\{x\in G_{\sigma}(R)\mid[x,E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)]\subset G_{\sigma}(J)\}.

Since
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\subset G_{\sigma}(J)
, we have
C_{\sigma}(R,J)\subset G_{\sigma}(R,J)
.
∎

7.
Proof of Theorem
1.4

Let
H
be a subgroup of
G_{\sigma}(R)
normalized by
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
. For
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}
, we write

J_{[\alpha]}(H)=\begin{cases}\{t\in R\mid x_{[\alpha]}(t)\in H\}&\text{if }[\alpha]\sim A_{1},A_{1}^{2},A_{1}^{3};\\
\{t\in R\mid\exists\ u\in R\text{ with }x_{[\alpha]}(t,u)\in H\text{ or }x_{[\alpha]}(u,t)\in H\}&\text{if }[\alpha]\sim A_{2}.\end{cases}

Define
J=\displaystyle\bigcup_{[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}}J_{[\alpha]}(H)
. To demonstrate the main theorem, we will first consider the following two propositions.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
J
be as above. Then

(1)

J
is a
\theta
-invariant ideal of
R
.

(2)

E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\subset H
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
J
be as above. Then

(1)

U_{\sigma}(R)\cap H\subset U_{\sigma}(J)
.

(2)

U_{\sigma}(rad(R))T_{\sigma}(R)U^{-}_{\sigma}(R)\cap H\subset U_{\sigma}(J)T_{\sigma}(R,J)U^{-}_{\sigma}(J)
, where
rad(R)
is the Jacobson radical of
R
.

The proofs of Propositions
7.1
and
7.2
can be found in Sections
8
and
9
, respectively.
Moving forward, let
\mathfrak{m}
be a maximal ideal of
R
and define
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}=\theta(\mathfrak{m})
. Set

S_{\mathfrak{m}}=\begin{cases}R\setminus\mathfrak{m}&\text{if }\mathfrak{m}=\bar{\mathfrak{m}},\\
R\setminus(\mathfrak{m}\cup\bar{\mathfrak{m}})&\text{if }\mathfrak{m}\neq\bar{\mathfrak{m}}\text{ and }o(\theta)=2,\\
R\setminus(\mathfrak{m}\cup\bar{\mathfrak{m}}\cup\bar{\bar{\mathfrak{m}}})&\text{if }\mathfrak{m}\neq\bar{\mathfrak{m}}\text{ and }o(\theta)=3.\end{cases}

Then
S_{\mathfrak{m}}
is a multiplicatively closed subset of
R
such that
\theta(S_{\mathfrak{m}})\subset S_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Therefore there is a natural automorphism of the ring
S_{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}R
induced by an automorphism
\theta
of
R
. We denote this automorphism of
S_{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}R
also by
\theta
. Let
\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}:G_{\sigma}(R)\longrightarrow G_{\sigma}(S_{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}R)
be the homomorphism of groups induced by the canonical homomorphism of rings from
R
to
S_{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}R
. We write
R_{S}
and
J_{S}
for
S_{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}R
and
S_{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}J
, respectively. By
S_{\theta}
, we mean
S_{\mathfrak{m}}\cap R_{\theta}
.

Proposition 7.3
.

\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(H)\subset G_{\sigma}(R_{S},J_{S})
.

The proof of Proposition
7.3
has been provided in Section
10
.

Proposition 7.4
.

For any element
g\in G_{\sigma}(R_{S},J_{S}),
there exists an elements
s\in S_{\theta}
such that

[\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(x_{[\alpha]}(s\cdot t)),g]\in\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J))

for all
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}
and
t\in R_{[\alpha]}.

Proof.

Note that
R_{S}
is a semi-local ring, and
J_{S}
is either contained in
rad(R_{S})
or equal to
R_{S}
.
If
J_{S}\subset\operatorname{rad}(R_{S})
, then by Proposition
5.4
and Corollary
5.9
, we have
G_{\sigma}(R_{S},J_{S})=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R_{S},J_{S})\,T_{\sigma}(R_{S},J_{S})
.
If
J_{S}=R_{S}
, then by Proposition
5.8
, it follows that
G_{\sigma}(R_{S},J_{S})=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R_{S},J_{S})\,T_{\sigma}(R_{S},J_{S})
.
In both cases, by Proposition
6.3
, we conclude that
G_{\sigma}(R_{S},J_{S})
is generated by all
h(\chi)\in T_{\sigma}(R_{S},J_{S})
and all the elements of the form

z_{[\beta]}(u,v):=x_{[\beta]}(u)\,x_{-[\beta]}(v)\,x_{[\beta]}(u)^{-1},

where
[\beta]\in\Phi_{\rho}
,
u\in(R_{S})_{[\beta]}
, and
v\in(J_{S})_{[\beta]}
. We first prove the lemma for each of these generators.

Let
g=h(\chi)\in T_{\sigma}(R_{S},J_{S})
where
\chi\in\text{Hom}_{1}(\Lambda_{\pi},R_{S}^{*})
. By Lemma
5.6
, we have
\chi(\alpha)\equiv 1
(mod
J_{S}
) for every root
\alpha\in\Phi
, that is,
1-\chi(\alpha)\in J_{S}
for every root
\alpha\in\Phi
. Hence, there exists
u_{\alpha}\in J
and
s_{\alpha}\in S
such that
1-\chi(\alpha)=\displaystyle\frac{u_{\alpha}}{s_{\alpha}}
. Take
s=\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi}s_{\alpha}\bar{s}_{\alpha}
or
\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi}s_{\alpha}\bar{s}_{\alpha}\bar{\bar{s}}_{\alpha}
depending on whether
o(\theta)=2
or
3
. Clearly,
s\in S_{\theta}
.
If
[\alpha]\sim A_{2}
and
t=(t_{1},t_{2})\in R_{[\alpha]}
, then
t/1
denotes
(t_{1}/1,t_{2}/1)
. Now

[\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(x_{[\alpha]}(s\cdot t)),g]=[x_{[\alpha]}(s/1\cdot t/1),h(\chi)]=x_{[\alpha]}(s/1\cdot t/1)x_{[\alpha]}(\chi(\alpha)s/1\cdot t/1)^{-1}.

If
[\alpha]\sim A_{1},A_{1}^{2}
or
A_{1}^{3}
then

[\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(x_{[\alpha]}(s\cdot t)),g]=x_{[\alpha]}\Big(\frac{st}{1}(1-\chi(\alpha))\Big)=x_{[\alpha]}\Big(\frac{stu_{\alpha}}{s_{\alpha}}\Big)=\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(x_{[\alpha]}(a_{\alpha,t})),

where
a_{\alpha,t}=\Bigg(\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\Phi\\
\beta\neq\alpha\end{subarray}}s_{\beta}\bar{s}_{\beta}\Bigg)\bar{s}_{\alpha}tu_{\alpha}
or
\Bigg(\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\Phi\\
\beta\neq\alpha\end{subarray}}s_{\beta}\bar{s}_{\beta}\bar{\bar{s}}_{\beta}\Bigg)\bar{s}_{\alpha}\bar{\bar{s}}_{\alpha}tu_{\alpha}
depending on whether
o(\theta)=2
or
3
.
Since
a_{\alpha,t}\in J_{[\alpha]}
, we obtain the required result in this case. Now if
[\alpha]\sim A_{2}
then

\displaystyle[\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(x_{[\alpha]}(s\cdot t)),g]

\displaystyle=x_{[\alpha]}\Bigg(\frac{st_{1}}{1},\frac{s^{2}t_{2}}{1}\Bigg)x_{[\alpha]}\Bigg(\chi(\alpha)\frac{st_{1}}{1},\chi(\alpha)\overline{\chi(\alpha)}\frac{s^{2}t_{2}}{1}\Bigg)^{-1}

\displaystyle=x_{[\alpha]}\Bigg((1-\chi(\alpha))\frac{st_{1}}{1},(1-\chi(\alpha))\frac{s^{2}t_{2}}{1}-\chi(\alpha)(1-\overline{\chi(\alpha)})\frac{s^{2}\bar{t}_{2}}{1}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=x_{[\alpha]}\Bigg(\frac{st_{1}u_{\alpha}}{s_{\alpha}},\frac{s^{2}t_{2}u_{\alpha}}{s_{\alpha}}-\frac{s^{2}\bar{t}_{2}(s_{\alpha}-u_{\alpha})\bar{u}_{\alpha}}{s_{\alpha}\bar{s}_{\alpha}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(x_{[\alpha]}(a_{\alpha,t},b_{\alpha,t})),

where
a_{\alpha,t}=\Bigg(\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\Phi\\
\beta\neq\alpha\end{subarray}}s_{\beta}\bar{s}_{\beta}\Bigg)\bar{s}_{\alpha}t_{1}u_{\alpha}
and
b_{\alpha,t}=\Bigg(\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\Phi\\
\beta\neq\alpha\end{subarray}}s_{\beta}\bar{s}_{\beta}\Bigg)\bar{s}_{\alpha}st_{2}u_{\alpha}-\Bigg(\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\Phi\\
\beta\neq\alpha\end{subarray}}s_{\beta}\bar{s}_{\beta}\Bigg)s\bar{t}_{2}(s_{\alpha}-u_{\alpha})\bar{u}_{\alpha}
. Since
(a_{\alpha,t},b_{\alpha,t})\in\mathcal{A}(J)
, we obtain the required result in this case.

Now let
g=z_{[\beta]}(u,v)
, where
[\beta]\in\Phi_{\rho},u\in(R_{S})_{[\beta]}
and
v\in(J_{S})_{[\beta]}
. We want to find
s\in S_{\theta}
such that

[x_{[\alpha]}(s/1\cdot t/1),z_{[\beta]}(u,v)]\in\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)),

for all
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}
and
t\in R_{[\alpha]}
.
If
[\beta]\sim A_{1},A_{1}^{2}
or
A_{1}^{3}
, then write
u=a/b
and
v=c/d
, where
a\in R,c\in J
and
b,d\in S_{\mathfrak{m}}
. If
[\beta]\sim A_{2}
, then write
u=(u_{1},u_{2})=(a_{1}/b_{1},a_{2}/b_{2})
and
v=(v_{1},v_{2})=(c_{1}/d_{1},c_{2}/d_{2})
, where
a_{1},a_{2}\in R,

c_{1},c_{2}\in J
and
b_{1},b_{2},d_{1},d_{2}\in S_{\mathfrak{m}}
.
Depending on the type of root
[\beta]
, we can choose the value of
s
as below:

s=\begin{cases}(bd)^{m}&\text{if }[\beta]\sim A_{1},\\
(b\bar{b}d\bar{d})^{m}&\text{if }[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{2},\\
(b\bar{b}\bar{\bar{b}}d\bar{d}\bar{\bar{d}})^{m}&\text{if }[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{3},\\
(b_{1}\bar{b}_{1}b_{2}\bar{b}_{2}d_{1}\bar{d}_{1}d_{2}\bar{d}_{2})^{m}&\text{if }[\beta]\sim A_{2},\end{cases}

for sufficiently large positive integer
m
. We proceed using a similar method as in Proposition
6.3
.

Case A.
[\alpha]\neq\pm[\beta]
.
Since no convex combination of the roots
[\alpha],-[\beta]
and
i[\alpha]+j[\beta]\ (i,j\neq 0)
is
0
, by using Chevalley commutator formula, we obtain

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt[x_{[\alpha]}(s/1\cdot t/1),z_{[\beta]}(u,v)]

\displaystyle=x_{[\alpha]}(s/1\cdot t/1)x_{[\beta]}(u)x_{-[\beta]}(v)x_{[\beta]}(u)^{-1}x_{[\alpha]}(s/1\cdot t/1)^{-1}x_{[\beta]}(u)x_{-[\beta]}(v)x_{[\beta]}(u)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Big(\prod x_{i[\alpha]+j[\beta]}(c_{i,j}(s,t,u,v))\Big),

where
c_{i,j}=c_{i,j}(s,t,u,v)
are functions of
s,t,u
and
v
such that
s
and
v
(or
\bar{v},v_{1},\bar{v}_{1},v_{2},\bar{v}_{2}
; the last four candidates appear only when
v=(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathcal{A}(R)
) appear in each term of
c_{i,j}
. But then, for sufficiently large
m
(see the definition of
s
), the values of
c_{i,j}(s,t,u,v)
are in
J_{i[\alpha]+j[\beta]}
, as desired.

Case B.
[\alpha]=\pm[\beta]
.
Since the rank of
\Phi_{\rho}>1
, there exists
[\gamma]\in\Phi_{\rho}
such that
[\beta]
and
[\gamma]
is base of connected subsystem
\Phi^{\prime}
of
\Phi_{\rho}
is of rank
2
.
By Lemma
6.4
, there exists
a\in(J_{S})_{[\beta]+[\gamma]}
and
b\in(R_{S})_{[\gamma]}
such that

x_{-[\beta]}(v)=[x_{-[\beta]-[\gamma]}(a),x_{[\gamma]}(b)]h^{\prime}

with
h^{\prime}=\prod_{i\leq 0,j<0}x_{i[\beta]+j[\gamma]}(a_{ij})
.
But then

\displaystyle z_{[\beta]}(u,v)

\displaystyle=x_{[\beta]}(u)x_{-[\beta]}(v)x_{[\beta]}(u)^{-1}

\displaystyle=x_{[\beta]}(u)\{[x_{-[\beta]-[\gamma]}(a),x_{[\gamma]}(b)]h^{\prime}\}x_{[\beta]}(u)^{-1}

\displaystyle=[x_{[\beta]}(u),x_{-[\beta]-[\gamma]}(a)]x_{-[\beta]-[\gamma]}(a)[x_{[\beta]}(u),x_{[\gamma]}(b)]x_{[\gamma]}(b)[x_{[\beta]}(u),x_{-[\beta]-[\gamma]}(a)^{-1}]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274ptx_{-[\beta]-[\gamma]}(a)^{-1}[x_{[\beta]}(u),x_{[\gamma]}(b)^{-1}]x_{[\gamma]}(b)^{-1}[x_{[\beta]}(u),h^{\prime}]h^{\prime}.

Now we consider

\displaystyle[x_{[\alpha]}(s/1\cdot t/1),z_{[\beta]}(u,v)]

\displaystyle=x_{\pm[\beta]}(s/1\cdot t/1)z_{[\beta]}(u,v)x_{\pm[\beta]}(s/1\cdot t/1)^{-1}z_{[\beta]}(u,v)^{-1}.

Using the above expression of
z_{[\beta]}(u,v)
and Chevalley commutator formulas, we can conclude that
[x_{[\alpha]}(s/1\cdot t/1),z_{[\beta]}(u,v)]
can be expressed as a product of elements of the form
x_{[\delta]}(c_{[\delta]})
, where
[\delta]\in\Phi^{\prime}
and
c_{[\delta]}\in(R_{S})_{[\delta]}
such that
s
and
v
(or
\bar{v},v_{1},\bar{v}_{1},v_{2},\bar{v}_{2}
) appear in each term of
c_{[\delta]}
. Consequently, for sufficiently large
m
, the values of
c_{[\delta]}
are in
J_{[\delta]}
, as desired.

Finally, let
g
be an arbitrary element of
G_{\sigma}(R_{S},J_{S})
. We aim to show that for any given
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}
and
t\in R_{[\alpha]}
, there exists
s\in S_{\theta}
such that

[\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(x_{[\alpha]}(s\cdot t)),g]\in\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)).

As noted earlier,
g
can be written as a product

g=x_{1}\cdots x_{n},

where each factor
x_{i}
is either of the form
h(\chi)
for some
\chi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{1}(\Lambda_{\pi},(R_{S})^{*})
, or
z_{[\beta]}(u,v)
with
[\beta]\in\Phi_{\rho}
,
u\in(R_{S})_{[\beta]}
and
v\in(J_{S})_{[\beta]}
.
We have already established that for each such
x_{i}
, there exists
s_{i}\in S_{\theta}
such that

[\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(x_{[\alpha]}(s_{i}\cdot t)),x_{i}]\in\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)).

Let
s=s_{1}\cdots s_{n}
and define
w=\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(x_{[\alpha]}(s\cdot t))
. A straightforward computation yields

[w,g]=[w,x_{1}\cdots x_{n}]=\{[w,x_{1}]\}\{{}^{x_{1}}[w,x_{2}]\}\{{}^{x_{1}x_{2}}[w,x_{3}]\}\cdots\{{}^{x_{1}\cdots x_{n-1}}[w,x_{n}]\},

where
{}^{a}b
denotes the conjugation
aba^{-1}
.
Using a similar (though slightly adapted) argument previously applied to each generator
x_{i}
, we conclude that

\{{}^{x_{1}\cdots x_{i-1}}[w,x_{i}]\}\in\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J))\quad\text{for each }i.

Therefore, the entire commutator
[w,g]
lies in
\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J))
, as required.
∎

Before proceeding further, we state a lemma from G. Taddei
[
22
]
. Let
G_{\pi}(\Phi,R)
be a Chevalley group over a commutative ring
R
. Consider
R[X]
, the polynomial ring in one variable
X
with coefficients in
R
. For a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
, let
\psi^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}:G_{\pi}(\Phi,R)\longrightarrow G_{\pi}(\Phi,R_{S})
denote the natural map induced by the canonical ring homomorphism
R\longrightarrow R_{S}
.

Lemma 7.5

(
[
22
, Lemma 3.14]
)
.

Let
\epsilon(X)
be an element of
G_{\pi}(\Phi,R[X])
. Suppose
\psi^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\epsilon(X))=1
and
\epsilon(0)=1
. Then there exists an element
s
of
S
such that
\epsilon(sX)=1
.

Remark
.

Consider a twisted Chevalley group
G_{\sigma}(R)
. Note that an automorphism
\theta:R\longrightarrow R
of order
n
(where
n=2
or
3
) can be naturally extended to an automorphism of
R[X]
of the same order, denoted also by
\theta
. Therefore, we can make sense of the group
G_{\sigma}(R[X])
.
Since
\psi^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}\mid_{G_{\sigma}(R)}=\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}
, we can apply the conclusion of Lemma
7.5
to the case of twisted Chevalley groups as well. Moreover, in this context, we can also choose
s\in S_{\theta}
(see
[
20
, Lemma
4.7
]
).

Proposition 7.6
.

For any maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
R
and
g\in H
, there exists
s\in S_{\theta}
such that

[x_{[\alpha]}(s\cdot t),g]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J),

for all
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}
and
t\in R_{[\alpha]}
.

Proof.

The canonical map
R\longrightarrow R_{S}
naturally induces the following maps:

\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}:G_{\sigma}(R)\longrightarrow G_{\sigma}(R_{S})\text{ and }\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}:G_{\sigma}(R[X])\longrightarrow G_{\sigma}(R_{S}[X]).

It is clear that
\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}\mid_{G_{\sigma}(R)}=\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}
.
Let
g\in H
. Then, by Proposition
7.3
,
\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(g)\in G_{\sigma}(R_{S},J_{S})\subset G_{\sigma}(R_{S}[X],J_{S}[X])
. By Proposition
7.4
, for the case of
\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}
, there exists
s^{\prime}\in S_{\theta}
such that

\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}([x_{[\alpha]}(s^{\prime}X\cdot t),g])\in\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R[X],J[X])),

for all
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}
and
t\in R_{[\alpha]}
. Moreover, from the proof of Proposition
7.4
, we can preciously write

\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}([x_{[\alpha]}(s^{\prime}X\cdot t),g])=\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(c_{i}(X))\Big),

where
c_{i}(X)\in(J[X])_{[\alpha_{i}]}
such that
c_{i}(0)=0
.
Put

\epsilon_{[\alpha]}(X)=[x_{[\alpha]}(s^{\prime}X\cdot t),g]\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(c_{i}(X))\Big)^{-1}.

Then we see that
\epsilon_{[\alpha]}(X)
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
7.5
, and hence there exists
s^{\prime}_{[\alpha]}\in S_{\theta}
such that
\epsilon_{[\alpha]}(s^{\prime}_{[\alpha]}X)=1
.
Thus we obtain

[x_{[\alpha]}(s^{\prime}(s^{\prime}_{[\alpha]}X)\cdot t),g]=\prod_{i=1}^{m}x_{[\alpha_{i}]}(c_{i}(s^{\prime}_{[\alpha]}X)).

Now by taking
X=1
and
s_{[\alpha]}=s^{\prime}s^{\prime}_{[\alpha]}
, we derive

[x_{[\alpha]}(s_{[\alpha]}\cdot t),g]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J).

Finally, if we set
s=\prod_{[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}}s_{[\alpha]}\in S_{\theta}
then
s
is the required element.
∎

Now we are in a position to prove the main theorem.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.
Let
H
be a subgroup of
G_{\sigma}(R)
normalized by
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
and let
J
be as earlier. Since
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\subset H
(see Proposition
7.1
), it only remains to prove that
H\subset G_{\sigma}(R,J)
. To demonstrate this, it suffices to show, by Corollary
6.8
, that if
g\in H
then
[x_{[\alpha]}(t),g]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
for every
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}
and
t\in R_{[\alpha]}
.

Case A.
[\alpha]\sim A_{1}
:
Define
I_{g,[\alpha]}=\{s\in R_{\theta}\mid[x_{[\alpha]}(st),g]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\text{ for all }t\in R_{\theta}\}
. Then
I_{g,[\alpha]}
is an ideal of
R_{\theta}
. To see this, let
s_{1},s_{2}\in I_{g,[\alpha]}
. Then for all
t\in R_{\theta}
,

[x_{[\alpha]}((s_{1}+s_{2})t),g]=(x_{[\alpha]}(s_{1}t)[x_{[\alpha]}(s_{2}t),g]x_{[\alpha]}(s_{1}t)^{-1})[x_{[\alpha]}(s_{1}t),g]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J).

Therefore,
s_{1}+s_{2}\in I_{g,[\alpha]}
. By the definition of
I_{g,[\alpha]}
, it is clear that for all
r\in R_{\theta}
, we have
rs_{1}\in I_{g,[\alpha]}
.
For any maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{\theta}
of
R_{\theta}
, by Proposition
7.6
and by Lemma
4.2
, there exists
s\in I_{g,[\alpha]}
such that
s\not\in\mathfrak{m}_{\theta}
. Thus, we can conclude that
I_{g,[\alpha]}=R_{\theta}
. But then
1\in I_{g,[\alpha]}
and hence
[x_{[\alpha]}(t),g]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
for all
t\in R_{\theta}
, as desired.

Case B.
[\alpha]\sim A_{1}^{2}
or
A_{1}^{3}
:
Define
I_{g,[\alpha]}=\{s\in R\mid[x_{[\alpha]}(st),g]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\text{ for all }t\in R\}
. Then, by a similar argument as in Case A, we can see that
I_{g,[\alpha]}
is an ideal of
R
. For any maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
R
, by Proposition
7.6
, there exists
s\in I_{g,[\alpha]}
such that
s\not\in\mathfrak{m}
. Thus, we can conclude that
I_{g,[\alpha]}=R
. But then
[x_{[\alpha]}(t),g]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
for all
t\in R
, as desired.

Case C.
[\alpha]\sim A_{2}
:
Define
A_{g,[\alpha]}=\{s\in R\mid[x_{[\alpha]}(st,s\bar{s}t\bar{t}/2),g]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\text{ for all }t\in R\}
. Let
I_{g,[\alpha]}^{(1)}
be the ideal of
R
generated by
A_{g,[\alpha]}
and
I_{g,[\alpha]}^{(2)}=\{s\in R_{\theta}\mid[x_{[\alpha]}(0,st),g]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\text{ for all }t\in R\text{ such that }t=-\bar{t}\}
.
We claim that
I_{g,[\alpha]}^{(2)}
is an ideal of
R_{\theta}
. To see this, let
s_{1},s_{2}\in I^{(2)}_{g,[\alpha]}
. Then for all
t\in R
such that
t=-\bar{t}
, we have

\displaystyle[x_{[\alpha]}(0,(s_{1}+s_{2})t),g]

\displaystyle=[x_{[\alpha]}(0,s_{1}t)x_{[\alpha]}(0,s_{2}t),g]

\displaystyle=(x_{[\alpha]}(0,s_{1}t)[x_{[\alpha]}(0,s_{2}t),g]x_{[\alpha]}(0,s_{1}t)^{-1})([x_{[\alpha]}(0,s_{1}t),g])

\displaystyle\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J).

Therefore,
s_{1}+s_{2}\in I^{(2)}_{g,[\alpha]}
. By the definition of
I^{(2)}_{g,[\alpha]}
, it is clear that for all
r\in R_{\theta}
, we have
rs\in I^{(2)}_{g,[\alpha]}
.

For any maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
R
, Proposition
7.6
ensures the existence of an element
s\in A_{g,[\alpha]}
such that
s\notin\mathfrak{m}
. Therefore, we conclude that
I_{g,[\alpha]}^{(1)}=R
.
Next, observe the following:

For any maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
R
and any
g\in H
, there exists
s\in S_{\theta}
such that

[x_{[\alpha]}(0,st),g]\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)

for all
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}
and all
t\in R
satisfying
t=-\bar{t}
.

The proof of the above statement follows the same argument as that of Proposition
7.6
and is therefore omitted.
Hence, we conclude that
I^{(2)}_{g,[\alpha]}=R_{\theta}
.

Let
(t,u)\in\mathcal{A}(R)
. Since
I^{(1)}_{g,[\alpha]}=R
, there exists
s_{1},\dots,s_{k}\in A_{g,[\alpha]}
and
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}\in R
such that
r_{1}s_{1}+\cdots+r_{k}s_{k}=1
. Therefore,
t=r_{1}s_{1}t+\cdots+r_{k}s_{k}t
. Observe that

\displaystyle(t,u)

\displaystyle=(r_{1}s_{1}t+\cdots+r_{k}s_{k}t,u)

\displaystyle=\left(r_{1}s_{1}t,\frac{r_{1}\bar{r}_{1}s_{1}\bar{s}_{1}t\bar{t}}{2}\right)\oplus\cdots\oplus\left(r_{k}s_{k}t,\frac{r_{k}\bar{r}_{k}s_{k}\bar{s}_{k}t\bar{t}}{2}\right)\oplus(0,c),

for some
c\in R
with
c=-\bar{c}
. Set

x_{i}=x_{[\alpha]}\left(r_{i}s_{i}t,\;\frac{r_{i}\bar{r}_{i}s_{i}\bar{s}_{i}t\bar{t}}{2}\right)\quad\text{for each }i=1,\dots,k,\quad\text{and}\quad x_{k+1}=x_{[\alpha]}(0,c).

Then
x_{[\alpha]}(t,u)=x_{1}\dots x_{k+1}
.
Since
s_{i}\in A_{g,[\alpha]}
, it follows that
x_{i}\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
for all
i=1,\dots,k
. Moreover, as
1\in I^{(2)}_{g,[\alpha]}
, we have
x_{k+1}\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle[x_{[\alpha]}(t,u),g]

\displaystyle=[x_{1}\dots x_{k+1},g]

\displaystyle=\{{}^{(x_{1}\cdots x_{k})}[x_{k+1},g]\}\{{}^{(x_{1}\cdots x_{k-1})}[x_{k},g]\}\cdots\{{}^{x_{1}}[x_{2},g]\}\{[x_{1},g]\}\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J),

as desired. ∎

Corollary 7.7
.

Let
R
and
\Phi
be as described in Theorem
1.4
. Then a subgroup
H
of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(\Phi,R)
is normal if and only if there exists a unique
\theta
-invariant ideal
J
of
R
such that

E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)\subset H\subset G_{\sigma}(R,J)\cap E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(\Phi,R).

Proof.

The result follows directly from Theorem
1.4
and Corollary
6.7
.
∎

8.
Proof of Proposition
7.1

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
R
be a ring with unity, and let
J
be a
\theta
-invariant ideal of
R
. Assume that
1/2\in R
. Consider the group
G=(\mathcal{A}(J),\oplus)
. Define the subgroups
H=\{(0,u)\mid u\in J,\bar{u}=-u\}
and
K=\langle(r,r\bar{r}/2)\mid r\in J\rangle
of
G
. Then
H
is a normal subgroup of
G
; in fact, it is contained in the centre of
G
, and
G=HK
. Moreover, if
J=R
then
H\subset K
, that is,
G=K
.

Proof.

The first assertion is clear as
(g_{1},g_{2})(0,u)(g_{1},g_{2})^{-1}=(0,u)
for appropriate
g_{1},g_{2},u\in J
. For the second assertion, note that for given
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathcal{A}(J)
, we have

(g_{1},g_{2})=(g_{1},g_{1}\bar{g_{1}}/2)(0,(g_{2}-\bar{g_{2}})/2).

Hence
G=HK
. Now if
J=R
, then we want to show that
H\subset K
.
Our work is done if we show that
(0,(g_{2}-\bar{g_{2}})/2)
is generated by elements of the form
(r,r\bar{r}/2),\ r\in R
, which follows from below:

(0,(g_{2}-\bar{g_{2}})/2)=(1+g_{2},(1+g_{2})(1+\bar{g_{2}})/2)^{-1}(1,1/2)(g_{2},g_{2}\bar{g_{2}}/2).

∎

Lemma 8.2
.

Assume that
1/2\in R
if
o(\theta)=2
and
1/3\in R
if
o(\theta)=3
. Let
z\in R
.

(1)

If
z=\bar{z}
, then
(Rz)_{\theta}=R_{\theta}z
.

(2)

If
o(\theta)=2
and
rz+r^{\prime}\bar{z}\in(Rz+R\bar{z})_{\theta}
, then there exists
t\in R
such that
rz+r^{\prime}\bar{z}=tz+\bar{t}\bar{z}.

(3)

If
o(\theta)=3
and
rz+r^{\prime}\bar{z}+r^{\prime\prime}\bar{\bar{z}}\in(Rz+R{\bar{z}}+R{\bar{\bar{z}}})_{\theta}
, then there exists
t\in R
such that
rz+r^{\prime}\bar{z}+r^{\prime\prime}\bar{\bar{z}}=tz+\overline{tz}+\overline{\overline{tz}}.

Proof.

Note that
(a)
follows from
(b)
(or
(c)
) and our assumption on
R
. To prove
(b)
, observe that
rz+r^{\prime}\bar{z}\in(Rz+R\bar{z})_{\theta}\implies\overline{(rz+r^{\prime}\bar{z})}=rz+r^{\prime}\bar{z}\implies(r-\bar{r^{\prime}})z=(\bar{r}-r^{\prime})\bar{z}.
Set
a:=r-\bar{r^{\prime}},
then
\bar{a}=\bar{r}-r^{\prime}
and
az=\overline{az}
. Now let
t=r-a/2=r/2+\bar{r^{\prime}}/2
, then
\bar{t}=\bar{r}-\bar{a}/2=r^{\prime}+\bar{a}/2
. Then
tz+\overline{tz}=(r-a/2)z+(r^{\prime}+\bar{a}/2)\bar{z}=rz+r^{\prime}\bar{z}+(\overline{az}-az)/2=rz+r^{\prime}\bar{z},
as desired. Similarly, to prove
(c)
, observe that
rz+r^{\prime}\bar{z}+r^{\prime\prime}\bar{\bar{z}}\in(Rz+R\bar{z}+R\bar{\bar{z}})_{\theta}\implies\overline{rz+r^{\prime}\bar{z}+r^{\prime\prime}\bar{\bar{z}}}=rz+r^{\prime}\bar{z}+r^{\prime\prime}\bar{\bar{z}}\implies\bar{r}\bar{z}+\bar{r^{\prime}}\bar{\bar{z}}+\bar{r^{\prime\prime}}z=rz+r^{\prime}\bar{z}+r^{\prime\prime}\bar{\bar{z}}\implies(r-\bar{r^{\prime\prime}})z+(r^{\prime}-\bar{r})\bar{z}+(r^{\prime\prime}-\bar{r^{\prime}})\bar{\bar{z}}=0.
Set
a:=r-\bar{r^{\prime\prime}},b:=r^{\prime}-\bar{r}
and
c:=r^{\prime\prime}-\bar{r^{\prime}}
, then
\bar{\bar{a}}+\bar{b}+c=0
and
az+b\bar{z}+c\bar{\bar{z}}=0
. Now let
t=r-a/3+\bar{\bar{b}}/3=(r+\bar{r^{\prime\prime}}+\bar{\bar{r^{\prime}}})/3
, then
\bar{t}=r^{\prime}-\bar{a}/3-2b/3
and
\bar{\bar{t}}=r^{\prime\prime}+2\bar{\bar{a}}/3+\bar{b}/3
. Then

\displaystyle tz+\overline{tz}+\overline{\overline{tz}}

\displaystyle=(r-a/3+\bar{\bar{b}}/3)z+(r^{\prime}-\bar{a}/3-2b/3)\bar{z}+(r^{\prime\prime}+2\bar{\bar{a}}/3+\bar{b}/3)\bar{\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=(rz+\overline{rz}+\overline{\overline{rz}})-2(az+b\bar{z}+(-\bar{\bar{a}}-\bar{b})\bar{\bar{z}})/3-(\bar{a}\bar{z}+\bar{b}\bar{\bar{z}}+(-a-\bar{\bar{b}})z)/3

\displaystyle=(rz+\overline{rz}+\overline{\overline{rz}})-2(az+b\bar{z}+c\bar{\bar{z}})/3-(\bar{a}\bar{z}+\bar{b}\bar{\bar{z}}+\bar{c}z)/3

\displaystyle=(rz+\overline{rz}+\overline{\overline{rz}}),

as desired.
∎

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
I
and
J
be
\theta
-invariant ideals of
R
. Then
\mathcal{A}(I+J)=\mathcal{A}(I)\mathcal{A}(J)=\mathcal{A}(J)\mathcal{A}(I)
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\mathcal{A}(I)\mathcal{A}(J)\subset\mathcal{A}(I+J)
. For converge, let
(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathcal{A}(I+J)
. Then
r_{1}\bar{r}_{1}=r_{2}+\bar{r}_{2}
and there exists
a_{1},a_{2}\in I
and
b_{1},b_{2}\in J
such that
r_{1}=a_{1}+b_{1}
and
r_{2}=a_{2}+b_{2}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle r_{1}\bar{r}_{1}=r_{2}+\bar{r}_{2}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle(a_{1}+b_{1})(\bar{a}_{1}+\bar{b}_{1})=(a_{2}+b_{2})+(\bar{a}_{2}+\bar{b}_{2})

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle a_{1}\bar{a}_{1}+a_{1}\bar{b}_{1}+b_{1}\bar{a}_{1}+b_{1}\bar{b}_{1}=(a_{2}+b_{2})+(\bar{a}_{2}+\bar{b}_{2}).

By using this, we can write

\displaystyle(r_{1},r_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}+b_{1},a_{2}+b_{2})

\displaystyle=\Big(a_{1},\frac{a_{1}\bar{a}_{1}+(a_{2}-\bar{a}_{2})}{2}\Big)\oplus\Big(b_{1},\frac{b_{1}\bar{b}_{1}+(b_{2}-\bar{b}_{2})}{2}\Big)\oplus\Big(0,\frac{a_{1}\bar{b}_{1}-\bar{a}_{1}b_{1}}{2}\Big).

Hence
(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathcal{A}(I)\mathcal{A}(J)
. Therefore
\mathcal{A}(I+J)=\mathcal{A}(I)\mathcal{A}(J)=\mathcal{A}(J)\mathcal{A}(I)
, later equality is possible because
\mathcal{A}(I)\mathcal{A}(J)
is a group.
∎

Proposition 8.4
.

Fix a root
[\alpha]\in\Phi_{\rho}
and an element
z\in R_{[\alpha]}
. Let
H
be the normal subgroup of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R)
generated by
x_{[\alpha]}(z)
. Then
H=E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
where

J=\begin{cases}Rz&\text{if }[\alpha]\sim A_{1},\\
Rz+R{\bar{z}}&\text{if }[\alpha]\sim A_{1}^{2},\\
Rz+R{\bar{z}}+R{\bar{\bar{z}}}&\text{if }[\alpha]\sim A_{1}^{3},\\
Rz_{1}+R{\bar{z_{1}}}+R(z_{2}-\bar{z_{2}})&\text{if }[\alpha]\sim A_{2}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Since
x_{[\alpha]}(z)\in E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
, by Proposition
6.1
, we have
H\subset E^{\prime}_{\sigma}(R,J)
. For the reverse inclusion, we need to prove that
x_{[\beta]}(t)\in H
for every
t\in J_{[\beta]}
and
[\beta]\in\Phi_{\rho}
. Observe that there exist a sequence of roots
[\alpha_{i}]\ (i=1,\dots,n)
such that
[\alpha_{1}]=[\alpha],[\alpha_{n}]=[\beta]
, and for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, the pair of roots
[\alpha_{i}],[\alpha_{i+1}]
contained in some connected subsystem of
\Phi_{\rho}
of rank
2
. Now, by applying Lemma
8.5
(below) recursively to the pairs
([\alpha_{i}],[\alpha_{i+1}])
, we obtain the desired result.
∎

Lemma 8.5
.

Let the notation be as established in Proposition
8.4
. Suppose
\Phi^{\prime}
is a connected subsystem of
\Phi_{\rho}
with rank 2. If
[\gamma]\in\Phi^{\prime}
and
s\in R
such that
x_{[\gamma]}(s)\in H
, then
x_{[\beta]}(t)\in H
for every
[\beta]\in\Phi^{\prime}
and
t\in I_{[\beta]}
, where
I
is an
\theta
-invariant ideal defined similarly to the ideal
J
in Proposition
8.4
by replacing
z
with
s
.

Proof.

Let
\mu
denote the angle between
[\beta]
and
[\gamma]
. For
r\in R
, let
r^{\prime}
denote either
r,\bar{r}
or
\bar{\bar{r}}
. By
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, we mean the first and second coordinate of
r=(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathcal{A}(R)
.
Consider the following table outlining the possible types of choices for
\Phi^{\prime}
:

Type

\Phi_{\rho}

Possible Choices of
\Phi^{\prime}

)

(containing long and short roots)

Case A.
\Phi^{\prime}\sim A_{2}
which contains roots of the type
A_{1}
.
This case arises only when
\Phi_{\rho}\sim{}^{2}D_{n}\ (n\geq 4),{}^{3}D_{4}
or
{}^{2}E_{6}
. Let us consider the following subcases:

(A1)

[\gamma]\sim A_{1}
and
\mu=\pi/3
.
In this case, the pair
[\gamma]
and
[\beta]-[\gamma]
is of type
(b-i)
. Here
[\gamma],[\beta]
and
[\beta]-[\gamma]
are of type
A_{1}
. For
r\in R_{\theta}
, we have

[x_{[\gamma]}(s),x_{[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r)]=x_{[\beta]}(rs)\in H.

(A2)

[\gamma]\sim A_{1}
and
\mu
is arbitrary.
Observe that, we can find a sequence
[\gamma_{1}],\dots,[\gamma_{m}]
of roots in
\Phi^{\prime}
such that
2\leq m\leq 6,[\gamma_{1}]=[\gamma],[\gamma_{m}]=[\beta]
and the angle between
[\gamma_{i}]
and
[\gamma_{i+1}]
is
\pi/3
for every
i=1,\dots,m-1
. Then, by applying Case (A1) to the pair of roots
([\gamma_{i-1}],[\gamma_{i}])
, we have
x_{[\gamma_{i}]}(rs)\in H
for every
r\in R_{\theta}
and
i=2,\dots,m
. In particular, by Lemma
8.2
,
x_{[\beta]}(t)\in H
for every
t\in I_{[\beta]}
, as desired.

Case B.
\Phi^{\prime}\sim A_{2}
which contains roots of the type
A_{1}^{2}
.
This case arises only when
\Phi_{\rho}\sim{}^{2}A_{2n-1}\ (n\geq 3){}^{2}A_{2n}\ (n\geq 3)
or
{}^{2}E_{6}
. Let us consider the following subcases:

(B1)

[\gamma]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
\mu=\pi/3
.
In this case, the pair
[\gamma]
and
[\beta]-[\gamma]
is of the type
(b-ii)
. Here
[\gamma],[\beta]
and
[\beta]-[\gamma]
are of type
A_{1}^{2}
. For
r\in R
, we have

[x_{[\gamma]}(s),x_{[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r^{\prime})]=x_{[\beta]}(rs^{\prime})\in H.

(B2)

[\gamma]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
\mu
is arbitrary.
Observe that, we can find a sequence
[\gamma_{1}],\dots,[\gamma_{m}]
of roots in
\Phi^{\prime}
such that
2\leq m\leq 6,[\gamma_{1}]=[\gamma],[\gamma_{m}]=[\beta]
and the angle between
[\gamma_{i}]
and
[\gamma_{i+1}]
is
\pi/3
for every
i=1,\dots,m-1
. Then, by applying Case (B1) to the pair of roots
([\gamma_{i-1}],[\gamma_{i}])
, we have
x_{[\gamma_{i}]}(rs^{\prime})\in H
for every
r\in R
and
i=2,\dots,m
. In particular,
x_{[\beta]}(rs^{\prime})\in H
for every
r\in R
.

Note that, we can find a subsystem
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
of
\Phi_{\rho}
of type
B_{2}
such that
[\beta]\in\Phi^{\prime\prime}
. Since
x_{[\beta]}(rs^{\prime})\in H
, by Case C (if
\Phi_{\rho}\sim{}^{2}A_{2n-1}
or
{}^{2}E_{6}
) and by Case D (if
\Phi_{\rho}\sim{}^{2}A_{2n}
) (see below), we have
x_{[\beta]}(\bar{r}\bar{s^{\prime}})\in H
for every
r\in R
. In particular,
x_{[\beta]}(rs)
and
x_{[\beta]}(r\bar{s})\in H
for every
r\in R
. Therefore,
x_{[\beta]}(t)\in H
for every
t\in I_{[\beta]}
, as desired.

Case C.
\Phi^{\prime}\sim B_{2}
which contains roots of the type
A_{1}
and
A_{1}^{2}
.
This case arises only when
\Phi_{\rho}\sim{}^{2}A_{2n-1}\ (n\geq 2),{}^{2}D_{n}\ (n\geq 4)
or
{}^{2}E_{6}
. Let us consider the following subcases:

(C1)

[\gamma]\sim A_{1}
and
\mu=\pi/4
.
In this case, the pair
[\gamma]
and
[\beta]-[\gamma]
is of the type
(d-i)
. Here
[\gamma],2[\beta]-[\gamma]\sim A_{1}
and
[\beta],[\beta]-[\gamma]\sim A_{1}^{2}
. For
r\in R
, we have

[x_{[\gamma]}(s),x_{[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r/2)]=x_{[\beta]}(rs/2)x_{2[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r\bar{r}s/4)\in H.

Now put
-r
instead of
r
, we get
x_{[\beta]}(-rs/2)x_{2[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r\bar{r}s/4)\in H
. But then

x_{[\beta]}(rs)=\{x_{[\beta]}(rs/2)x_{2[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r\bar{r}s/4)\}\{x_{[\beta]}(-rs/2)x_{2[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r\bar{r}s/4)\}^{-1}\in H.

(C2)

[\gamma]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
\mu=\pi/4
.
In this case, the pair
[\gamma]
and
[\beta]-[\gamma]
is of the type
(c-i)
. Here
[\gamma],[\beta]-[\gamma]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
[\beta]\sim A_{1}
. For
r\in R
, we have

[x_{[\gamma]}(s),x_{[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r)]=x_{[\beta]}(r\bar{s}+\bar{r}s)\in H.

(C3)

[\gamma]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
\mu=\pi/2
.
In this case, the pair
[\gamma]
and
[\beta]-[\gamma]
is of the type
(d-i)
. Here
[\gamma],[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
[\beta]+[\gamma],[\beta]-[\gamma]\sim A_{1}
. For
r\in R_{\theta}
, we have

[x_{[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r),x_{[\gamma]}(s)]=x_{[\beta]}(rs)x_{[\gamma]+[\beta]}(\pm rs\bar{s})\in H.

Now, by Case (C2), we know that
x_{[\gamma]+[\beta]}(\pm rs\bar{s})\in H
. Hence

x_{[\beta]}(rs)=\{x_{[\beta]}(rs)x_{[\gamma]+[\beta]}(\pm rs\bar{s})\}\{x_{[\gamma]+[\beta]}(\pm rs\bar{s})\}^{-1}\in H.

In particular,
x_{[\beta]}(s)\in H
. By Case (C1) and Case (C2), we have
x_{[\beta]}(s+\bar{s})\in H
. But then
x_{[\beta]}(\bar{s})\in H
. Now, by switching roles of
{[\beta]}
and
[\gamma]
, we get
x_{[\gamma]}(\bar{s})\in H
. If we replace
s
by
\bar{s}
in above argument, then we get
x_{[\beta]}(r\bar{s})\in H
, for every
r\in R_{\theta}
.

(C4)

[\gamma]
and
\mu
are arbitrary.
Observe that, we can find a sequence
[\gamma_{1}],\dots,[\gamma_{m}]
of roots in
\Phi^{\prime}
such that
2\leq m\leq 8,[\gamma_{1}]=[\gamma],[\gamma_{m}]=[\beta]
and the angle between
[\gamma_{i}]
and
[\gamma_{i+1}]
is
\pi/4
for every
i=1,\dots,m-1
.

(a)

If
[\gamma]\sim A_{1}
and
[\beta]\sim A_{1}
then
m
must be odd. By applying Case (C1) to pairs
([\gamma_{i-1}],[\gamma_{i}])
, Case (C2) to pairs
([\gamma_{i}],[\gamma_{i+1}])
for
i=2,4,\dots,m-1
, inductively, we get
x_{[\beta]}((r+\bar{r})s)\in H
for every
r\in R
. Now by Lemma
8.2
, we have
x_{[\beta]}(t)\in H
for every
t\in I_{[\beta]}
.

(b)

If
[\gamma]\sim A_{1}
and
[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{2}
then
m
must be even. Note that
[\gamma_{m-1}]
is of type
A_{1}
. Hence, by the above case, we have
x_{[\gamma_{m-1}]}(s)\in H
. Now by applying Case (C1) to the pair
([\gamma_{m-1}],[\gamma_{m}])
, we conclude that
x_{[\beta]}(t)\in H
for every
t\in I_{[\beta]}
.

(c)

If
[\gamma]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
[\beta]\sim A_{1}
then
m
must be even. By applying Case (C3) to pairs
([\gamma_{i}],[\gamma_{i+2}])
for
i=1,3,\dots,m-3
, inductively, we get
x_{[\gamma_{m-1}]}(s)\in H
. Now by applying Case (C2) to the pair
([\gamma_{m-1}],[\gamma_{m}])
, we get
x_{[\beta]}(r\bar{s}+\bar{r}s)\in H
for every
r\in R
. Finally by Lemma
8.2
, we have
x_{[\beta]}(t)\in H
for all
t\in I_{[\beta]}
.

(d)

If
[\gamma]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{2}
then
m
must be odd. By applying Case (C2) to pairs
([\gamma_{i-1}],[\gamma_{i}])
, Case (C1) to pairs
([\gamma_{i}],[\gamma_{i+1}])
for
i=2,4,\dots,m-1
, inductively, we get
x_{[\beta]}(r_{1}(r_{2}\bar{s}+\bar{r_{2}}s))\in H
for every
r_{1},r_{2}\in R
. Now by applying Case (C3) to pairs
([\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i+2}])
for
i=1,3,\dots,m-2,
inductively, we get
x_{[\beta]}(r_{3}s)
and
x_{[\beta]}(r_{4}\bar{s})\in H
for every
r_{3},r_{4}\in R_{\theta}
.
But then for every
r_{5}\in R
, we have

x_{[\beta]}(r_{5}^{2}s)=x_{[\beta]}(r_{5}(r_{5}s+\bar{r_{5}}\bar{s}))\{x_{[\beta]}(r_{5}\bar{r_{5}}\bar{s})\}^{-1}\in H.

Similarly, we have
x_{[\beta]}(r_{6}^{2}\bar{s})\in H
for every
r_{6}\in R
.
Finally, for given
r\in R
we have

x_{[\beta]}(rs)=x_{[\beta]}\Big(\Big(\frac{1+r}{2}\Big)^{2}s\Big)\Big\{x_{[\beta]}\Big(\Big(\frac{1-r}{2}\Big)^{2}s\Big)\Big\}^{-1}\in H.

Similarly, we have
x_{[\beta]}(r\bar{s})\in H
for every
r\in R
. By Lemma
8.2
, we conclude that
x_{[\beta]}(t)\in H
for all
t\in I_{[\beta]}.

Case D.
\Phi^{\prime}\sim B_{2}
which contains roots of the type
A_{1}^{2}
and
A_{2}
.
This case arises only when
\Phi_{\rho}\sim{}^{2}A_{2n}\ (n\geq 2)
. Let us consider the following subcases:

(D1)

[\gamma]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
\mu=\pi/4
.
First observe that the pair
[\gamma]
and
[\beta]-[\gamma]
is of the type
(d-ii)
. Here
[\gamma],2[\beta]-[\gamma]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
[\beta],[\beta]-[\gamma]\sim A_{2}
. In this case, for
r=(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathcal{A}(R)
we have

[x_{[\gamma]}(s),x_{[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r_{1}^{\prime}/2,r_{2}^{\prime}/4)]=x_{[\beta]}(r_{1}s^{\prime}/2,r_{2}s\bar{s}/4)x_{2[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r_{2}^{\prime}s/4)\in H.

By putting
-r_{1}
instead of
r_{1}
, we get
x_{[\beta]}(-r_{1}s^{\prime}/2,r_{2}s\bar{s}/4)x_{2[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r_{2}^{\prime}s/4)\in H
. But then

\begin{split}x_{[\beta]}(r_{1}s^{\prime},\frac{r_{1}\bar{r_{1}}}{2}s\bar{s})&=x_{[\beta]}(r_{1}s^{\prime},\frac{r_{2}+\bar{r_{2}}}{2}s\bar{s})\\
&=\{x_{[\beta]}(r_{1}s^{\prime}/2,r_{2}s\bar{s}/4)x_{2[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r_{2}s/4)\}\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\{x_{[\beta]}(-r_{1}s^{\prime}/2,r_{2}s\bar{s}/4)x_{2[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r_{2}s/4)\}^{-1}\in H.\end{split}

Now observe that the pair
[\gamma]
and
2[\beta]-[\gamma]
is of type
(a_{2}-ii)
. Here
[\gamma],[\delta]:=2[\beta]-[\gamma]\sim A_{1}^{2}
and
[\beta]=1/2([\gamma]+[\delta])\sim A_{2}
. In this case, for
r\in R
we have

[x_{[\gamma]}(s),x_{[\delta]}(\pm r)]=x_{[\beta]}(0,\bar{r}s-r\bar{s})\in H.

We now claim that for every
(r_{3}s^{\prime},r_{4}s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}(Rs^{\prime})
, the element
x_{[\beta]}(r_{3}s^{\prime},r_{4}s^{\prime})\in H
. As in proof of Lemma
8.1
, we consider the following decomposition of
(r_{3}s^{\prime},r_{4}s^{\prime}):

(r_{3}s^{\prime},r_{4}s^{\prime})=(r_{3}s^{\prime},r_{3}\bar{r_{3}}s\bar{s}/2)\oplus(0,(r_{4}s^{\prime}-\overline{r_{4}s^{\prime}})/2).

Therefore, we have

x_{[\beta]}(r_{3}s^{\prime},r_{4}s^{\prime})=x_{[\beta]}(r_{3}s^{\prime},r_{3}\bar{r_{3}}s\bar{s}/2)x_{[\beta]}(0,(r_{4}s^{\prime}-\overline{r_{4}s^{\prime}})/2)\in H.

Finally, by Proposition
3.11
(or by Proposition
4.1
of
[
1
]
), we have

(2)

w_{2[\beta]-[\gamma]}(1)x_{[\gamma]}(s){w_{2[\beta]-[\gamma]}(1)}^{-1}=x_{[\gamma]}(-\bar{s}).

Therefore,
x_{[\gamma]}(-\bar{s})\in H
and hence
x_{[\gamma]}(\bar{s})\in H.
Replacing
s
by
\bar{s}
in above, we get
x_{[\beta]}(r_{3}\bar{s^{\prime}},r_{4}\bar{s^{\prime}})\in H
for every
(r_{3}\bar{s^{\prime}},r_{4}\bar{s^{\prime}})\in\mathcal{A}(R\bar{s^{\prime}})
. In particular, we have
x_{[\beta]}(r_{3}s,r_{4}s)\in H
(resp.,
x_{[\beta]}(r_{3}\bar{s},r_{4}\bar{s})\in H
) for every
(r_{3}s,r_{4}s)\in\mathcal{A}(Rs)
(resp.,
(r_{3}\bar{s},r_{4}\bar{s})\in\mathcal{A}(R\bar{s})
).

(D2)

[\gamma]\sim A_{2}
and
\mu=\pi/4
.
Note that, the pair
[\gamma]
and
[\beta]-[\gamma]
is of the type
(c-ii)
. Here
[\gamma],[\beta]-[\gamma]\sim A_{2}
and
[\beta]\sim A_{1}^{2}
. For
r\in R
, we have

(3)

[x_{[\gamma]}(s_{1},s_{2}),x_{[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\pm r^{\prime},r\bar{r}/2)]=x_{[\beta]}(rs_{1}^{\prime})\in H.

By (
2
), we also have
x_{[\beta]}(\bar{r}\bar{s_{1}^{\prime}})\in H
. In particular, for every
r\in R
, we have
x_{[\beta]}(rs_{1})\in H
and
x_{[\beta]}(r\bar{s_{1}})\in H
.

Observe that, the pair
[\gamma]
and
[\beta]-2[\gamma]
is of the type
(d-ii)
. Here
[\beta]-[\gamma],[\gamma]\sim A_{2}
and
[\beta],[\beta]-2[\gamma]\sim A_{1}^{2}
. For
r\in R
we have

(4)

[x_{[\beta]-2[\gamma]}(\pm r^{\prime}),x_{[\gamma]}(s_{1},s_{2})]=x_{[\beta]-[\gamma]}(rs_{1}^{\prime},r\bar{r}s_{2}^{\prime})x_{[\beta]}(\pm r^{\prime}s_{2}^{\prime\prime})\in H.

Note that,
(x_{[\gamma]}(s_{1},s_{2}))^{-1}=x_{[\gamma]}(-s_{1},\bar{s_{2}})\in H
. By above, we have
x_{[\beta]}(s_{1})\in H
. By applying case (D1) to the pair
([\beta],[\gamma])
, we have
x_{[\gamma]}(2s_{1},2s_{1}\bar{s_{1}})\in H
. But then

x_{[\gamma]}(s_{1},\bar{s_{2}})=x_{[\gamma]}(-s_{1},\bar{s_{2}})x_{[\gamma]}(2s_{1},2s_{1}\bar{s_{1}})\in H.

Put
(s_{1},\bar{s_{2}})
instead of
(s_{1},s_{2})
and
-r
instead of
r
in (
4
), we get

x_{[\beta]-[\gamma]}(-rs_{1}^{\prime},r\bar{r}\bar{s_{2}^{\prime}})x_{[\beta]}(\pm(-r^{\prime})\bar{s_{2}^{\prime\prime}})\in H.

Since the elements
x_{[\beta]-[\gamma]}(\cdot)
and
x_{[\beta]}(\cdot)
are commutes with each other, we have

\displaystyle x_{[\beta]}(\pm r^{\prime}(s_{2}^{\prime\prime}-\bar{s_{2}^{\prime\prime}}))

\displaystyle=\{x_{[\beta]-[\gamma]}(rs_{1}^{\prime},r\bar{r}s_{2}^{\prime})x_{[\beta]}(\pm r^{\prime}s_{2}^{\prime\prime})\}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\{x_{[\beta]-[\gamma]}(-rs_{1}^{\prime},r\bar{r}\bar{s_{2}^{\prime}})x_{[\beta]}(\pm(-r^{\prime})\bar{s_{2}^{\prime\prime}})\}\in H.

In particular, we have
